<meta>This page features the title of the work as presented on an ornate, 16th-century style leather binding. The original Latin title is embossed in gold, matching the intricate craftsmanship of the cover.</meta>

# THE HOLY
# BIBLE
# IN LATIN

<note>original: "Biblia Sacra Latina". This refers to the Vulgate, the version of the Bible translated into Latin by Saint Jerome in the 4th century, which became the standard text for the Western Church.</note>

<term>Bookbinding<gloss>The physical assembly of a book, which in this era was often a high art form involving wood boards covered in treated leather.</gloss></term>
<term>Gold tooling<gloss>A decorative process where a thin layer of gold leaf is pressed into the leather using heated brass stamps or "tools" to create shimmering designs.</gloss></term>
<term>Strapwork<gloss>A pattern of decorative bands that look like interlaced leather straps or ribbons, popular in Renaissance design.</gloss></term>
<term>Arabesque<gloss>A flowing decorative motif inspired by plant life, featuring winding stems and delicate leaves.</gloss></term>

<summary>This page presents the title of a Latin Holy Bible, preserved in a magnificent Renaissance-style binding featuring complex gold-tooled patterns. The craftsmanship indicates this was a high-status object intended for a library of significant importance.</summary>

<keywords>Holy Bible, Latin Vulgate, bookbinding, Renaissance art, gold tooling, strapwork, sacred text</keywords>

--- Page 2 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the physical description of the Bible's 16th-century binding to the actual beginning of the text. It contains the "General Prologue" written by Saint Jerome, which traditionally preceded his Latin translation of the Bible (the Vulgate). Jerome's letter is addressed to Paulinus, a fellow priest and later Bishop of Nola.</meta>

# Here begins the letter of Saint Jerome to Paulinus,
# priest, concerning all the books of divine history.
### Chapter one

**B**rother Ambrose, in bringing me your little gifts, delivered at the same time your most delightful letters, which, from the very beginning of our friendship, offered a sign of now-proven faith and of an old friendship. For that is a true bond <note>original: *necessitudo*; referring to a deep, necessary connection or kinship</note>, joined by the "glue of Christ," which is not brought together by household utility, nor by the mere presence of bodies, nor by a deceitful and stroking flattery, but rather by the fear of God and the study of the divine scriptures. We read in ancient histories that certain men traveled through provinces, approached new peoples, and crossed seas, so that they might see face to face those whom they had known from books. Thus Pythagoras [visited] the Memphis priests; thus Plato went to Egypt, and Archytas to Tarentum, and that coast of Italy which was once called "Greater Greece" <note>Magna Graecia</note>, most laboriously traversing it; and so that he who was a master at Athens and powerful—whose doctrines the gymnasiums of the Academy made to resound—might become a pilgrim and a disciple, preferring to learn others' teachings modestly rather than to impudently thrust his own forward. Finally, while he pursued letters as if they were fleeing across the whole world, <margin>end</margin> captured by pirates and sold, he obeyed a most cruel tyrant, led away as a captive, bound, and a slave; yet, because he was a philosopher, he was greater than the one who bought him. To Titus Livy, flowing from a "milky fountain of eloquence," we read that certain noblemen came from the furthest reaches of Spain and Gaul; and those whom Rome had not drawn to its own contemplation, the fame of a single man led there. That age possessed a miracle unheard of in all centuries and worthy of celebration: that having entered such a great city, they sought something else outside the city. Apollonius—whether he was that "magician" as the common folk say, or a "philosopher" as the Pythagoreans hand down—entered Persia, crossed the Caucasus, [visited] the Albanians, Scythians, Massagetae, and penetrated the most opulent kingdoms of India; and at last, having crossed the widest river, the Pison <note>A river mentioned in Genesis, often identified by ancient writers as the Ganges or Indus</note>, he reached the Brahmans, so that he might hear Iarchas sitting upon a golden throne and drinking from the fountain of Tantalus, teaching among a few disciples about nature, about morals, and about the course of days and stars. Then, returning through the Elamites, Babylonians, Chaldeans, Medes, Assyrians, Parthians, Syrians, Phoenicians, Arabs, [and] Palestinians, to Alexandria, he proceeded to Ethiopia, so that he might see the gymnosophists <note>literally "naked philosophers"; a sect of ancient Indian ascetics</note> and the most famous "Table of the Sun" in the sands. Everywhere that man found what he might learn; and always progressing, he always became better than himself. Philostratus wrote most fully about this in eight volumes. <insert>**chap. ii.**</insert>

**W**hy should I speak of men of the world, when the Apostle Paul, the "vessel of election" and teacher of the nations—who spoke from the consciousness of such a great guest within him, saying: "Do you seek a proof of Christ, who speaks in me?"—after traversing Damascus and Arabia, went up to Jerusalem to see Peter and stayed with him for fifteen days? For by this mystery of the "seven" and "eight" <note>original: *ebdomadis et ogdoadis*; Jerome notes that 15 days is the sum of 7 and 8, numbers of great symbolic significance in scripture representing the Sabbath and the Resurrection</note>, the future preacher of the nations was to be instructed. And again, after fourteen years, having taken Barnabas and Titus, he laid the Gospel before the apostles, lest perhaps he should run, or had run, in vain. The act of the "living voice" <note>original: *vivae vocis*</note> possesses I know not what hidden energy; and those things poured into the ears of a disciple from the mouth of the author sound more strongly. Hence also when Aeschines was in exile at Rhodes, and was reading that [speech] of Demosthenes...

<summary>This page contains the opening of Saint Jerome's famous prologue to the Bible, where he argues that true wisdom requires diligent study and personal travel, citing both pagan philosophers and the Apostle Paul as examples of those who sought truth across the world.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Jerome, Paulinus of Nola, Vulgate Prologue, Pythagoras, Plato, Apollonius of Tyana, Apostle Paul, sacred study, travel for wisdom</keywords>

--- Page 3 ---
<meta>This page continues Saint Jerome’s "Letter to Paulinus" (Epistle 53). Jerome has just finished a story about the Greek orator Aeschines to illustrate that reading a text is never as powerful as hearing the author speak it. Now, he transitions into a discussion on the necessity of studying the Scriptures deeply rather than relying on "holy simplicity" alone.</meta>

# . Letter .
### Chapter 3

...the speech which he had delivered against him: while all were wondering and praising it, he said with a sigh: "What if you had heard the beast himself roaring his own words?" <note>Jerome is quoting Aeschines regarding his rival Demosthenes, suggesting that even a great written speech is only a shadow of the living voice.</note> **Chapter 3**

<margin>Let him choose thrice</margin> <note>This marginal note "Ter eligat" likely refers to a selection process for readings or perhaps a mnemonic for the reader.</note>
**N**or do I say this because there is anything in me of such a kind that you could either hear from me or wish to learn; but because your ardor and zeal for learning ought to be proven by its own merit even without us. A teachable mind is praiseworthy even without a teacher. We do not consider what you find, but what you seek. Soft wax is easy to mold, even if the hands of the craftsman and modeler are absent; nevertheless, by its own potential, it is already everything it can become. 

The Apostle Paul glories that he learned the law of Moses and the prophets at the feet of Gamaliel, so that, armed with spiritual weapons, he might afterwards teach with confidence. "For the weapons of our warfare are not carnal, but powerful to God for the destruction of strongholds, destroying thoughts and every high thing that exalts itself against the knowledge of God, and bringing into captivity every understanding to the obedience of Christ, and being ready to subjugate all disobedience." <note>Jerome is quoting 2 Corinthians 10:4-6.</note> He writes that Timothy was instructed in the sacred scriptures from infancy, and he urges him to the study of reading, lest he neglect the grace given to him through the laying on of the hands of the priesthood. He commands Titus that, among the other virtues of a bishop—which he depicted in a brief discourse—he should not neglect the knowledge of the scriptures, saying that he must "hold fast that faithful word which is according to doctrine, that he may be able to exhort in sound doctrine and to convince those who contradict it." **Chapter 4**

**H**oly simplicity <note>original: *sancta rusticitas*; refers to the idea of being a "simple" or uneducated but holy person</note> indeed profits only itself; and as much as it builds up the Church of Christ by the merit of its life, it does just as much harm if it does not resist those who seek to destroy it. The prophet Malachi—rather, the Lord through Malachi—questioned the priests about the Law. It is the duty of the priest's office to respond when questioned about the Law. <column-break/> And in <margin>Deuteronomy</margin> Deuteronomy we read: "Ask your father and he will announce it to you; your elders and they will tell you." Also in the hundred and eighteenth Psalm: "Your justifications were my songs in the place of my pilgrimage." And in the description of the righteous man, when David compares him to the tree of life which is in paradise, among other virtues he added this: "In the law of the Lord is his will, and in His law will he meditate day and night." 

Daniel, at the end of his most sacred vision, says that the righteous shine like stars, and the "understanding"—that is, the learned—like the firmament. You see how much distance there is between holy simplicity and learned righteousness; some are compared to stars, others to the heavens. Although according to the "Hebrew truth" <note>original: *hebraicam veritatem*; Jerome’s preferred term for the original Hebrew text of the Old Testament</note>, both descriptions can be understood as referring to the learned. For thus we read among them: "But they that are learned shall shine as the brightness of the firmament; and they that instruct many to righteousness, as stars for all eternity." 

Why is Paul the Apostle called a "vessel of election"? <note>original: *vas electionis*; often translated as "chosen vessel"</note> Surely because he was a vessel of the Law and a storehouse of the holy scriptures. The Pharisees are amazed at the Lord's doctrine, and they marvel at Peter and John, wondering how they know the Law since they have not learned letters. For whatever training and daily meditation in the Law usually grants to others, the Holy Spirit suggested to them at that time; and they were, according to what is written, "taught of God." The Savior had completed twelve years, and sitting in the temple, questioning about the problems of the Law, He teaches more while He prudently questions. Unless perhaps we say that Peter and John were "unlearned"; both of whom could say: "Even if I be unskilled in speech, yet not in knowledge." John, a simple man, an unlearned fisherman—and from where, I ask you, comes that voice: "In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God"? *The Word* <note>original: *Logos*; the Greek term for Word, Reason, or Discourse</note> in Greek means many things...

<summary>Jerome argues that personal holiness ("holy simplicity") is insufficient for church leaders; they must also be deeply learned in the Scriptures to defend the faith against critics. He cites the examples of Paul, Timothy, and the Apostles to show that true spiritual authority combines divine inspiration with rigorous study.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Jerome, Paulinus of Nola, education, Holy Simplicity, Saint Paul, Gamaliel, Hebrew Truth, Logos, Biblical study, priesthood</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page continues Saint Jerome’s "Letter to Paulinus" (Epistle 53). In the previous section, Jerome emphasized that the "living voice" of a teacher carries more weight than the written word. Here, he begins to explain that the Bible is a "sealed book" that requires divine wisdom and guidance to unlock, contrasting worldly philosophy with Christian revelation.</meta>

# . Letter .
### Chapter 5

...means. For the "Word" is both Word <note>original: "verbum"</note> and Reason <note>original: "ratio"</note>: and a reckoning: and the cause of each individual thing through which all things subsist: all of which we rightly understand as being in Christ. **Chapter 5**

# The learned Plato did not know this; the eloquent Demosthenes was ignorant of it.
"I will destroy," he says, "the wisdom of the wise, and the prudence of the prudent I will reject." <note>Jerome is quoting 1 Corinthians 1:19, which in turn cites Isaiah 29:14.</note> True wisdom shall destroy false wisdom; and although the "foolishness of preaching" is found in the cross, nevertheless Paul speaks wisdom among the perfect. But it is not the wisdom of this world, <margin>Ambiguity that destroys</margin> which is being destroyed, nor of the princes of this world; rather, he speaks the wisdom of God hidden in mystery, which God predestined before the ages. God’s wisdom is Christ. For Christ is the power of God and the wisdom of God. This wisdom is hidden in mystery, of which also the title of the ninth Psalm is noted: "for the secrets of the son," <note>original: "pro occultis filii". In the Vulgate, this is the superscription of Psalm 9.</note> in whom are all the treasures of the wisdom and knowledge of God hidden. And He who was hidden in mystery was predestined before the ages: predestined and prefigured in the Law and the Prophets. Wherefore the prophets were also called "seers," <note>original: "videntes"</note> because they saw Him whom others did not see. Abraham saw His day and rejoiced. The heavens were opened to Ezekiel, which were closed to his own sinful people. "Open my eyes," says David, "and I will consider the wonders of your law." <note>Psalm 119:18.</note> For the Law is spiritual, and there is need of revelation so that it may be understood; and with the face revealed, they contemplate the glory of God. The book in the Apocalypse is shown sealed with seven seals; if you give it to a man skilled in letters <note>meaning a scholar or someone literate</note> to read, he will answer you: "I cannot, for it is sealed." How many today think they know letters, yet they hold a sealed book, nor can they open it unless He unlocks it who has the key of David—who opens and no one shuts, who shuts and no one opens. In the Acts

<column-break/>

of the Apostles, the holy eunuch—indeed, a holy man, for so Sacred Scripture names him—when he was reading the prophet Isaiah, was asked by Philip: "Do you think you understand what you are reading?" He answered: "How can I, unless someone has taught me?" I, to speak of myself, am neither holier than this eunuch, nor more studious; who came from Ethiopia, that is, from the furthest reaches of the world, to the temple; he left the royal court; and was such a lover of the law and of divine knowledge that he even read the sacred letters in his carriage. And yet, while he held the book and conceived the words of the Lord in his thoughts, turned them with his tongue, and sounded them with his lips, he was ignorant of Him whom he unknowingly worshipped in the book. Philip came: he showed him Jesus, who lay hidden, enclosed in the letter. O wonderful power of the teacher! In the same hour the eunuch believed; he was baptized as a faithful and holy man; and he immediately became a teacher from a disciple. He found more in a desert fountain of the Church than he had found in the gilded temple of the synagogue. **Chapter 6**

# These things have been briefly touched upon by me; for the narrowness of a letter
did not allow for wandering further; so that you might understand that you cannot enter into the Holy Scriptures without a guide <note>original: "previo", a scout or leader</note> and one who points out the path. I am silent about grammarians, rhetoricians, philosophers, geometers, logicians <note>original: "dyaleticis"</note>, musicians, astronomers, astrologers, and doctors—whose knowledge is sufficiently useful to mortals; and is divided into three parts: teaching, reason, and practice. I shall come to the lesser arts, <note>known as the "mechanical arts" in the Middle Ages, involving manual labor</note> and those which are administered not so much by the tongue as by the hand. Farmers, masons, smiths of metal and of wood, weavers, wool-workers also and fullers, and others who fashion various furniture and cheap small works, cannot be what they desire without a teacher. "Doctors promise what belongs to doctors; smiths handle the tools of smiths." <note>Jerome is quoting a Roman proverb from Horace.</note> Only the art of the Scriptures is...

<summary>Jerome argues that the Bible is a sealed mystery that requires a teacher to interpret, using the biblical story of Philip and the Ethiopian eunuch to show that even a devoted reader needs guidance. He contrasts the complexity of Scripture with the liberal and mechanical arts, all of which require training.</summary>
<keywords>interpretation of scripture, Ethiopian eunuch, Philip the Apostle, wisdom vs. knowledge, liberal arts, mechanical arts, Saint Jerome, keys of David</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues Saint Jerome’s "Letter to Paulinus" (Epistle 53). Jerome has just argued that the scriptures are a "sealed book" requiring divine grace to understand. He now turns his critique toward those who presume to teach the Bible without proper study, comparing their misinterpretations to the literary "centos" of the classical world before beginning a brief survey of the books of the Old Testament.</meta>

# . Letter .

...which everyone everywhere claims for themselves.
**L**earned and unlearned alike, we all write poems everywhere. <note>Jerome is quoting the Roman poet Horace (*Epistles* 2.1.117) to mock how everyone thinks they are a literary expert.</note> **T**he talkative old woman, the doting old man, the wordy sophist—everyone presumes to master this art; they tear it apart and teach it before they have even learned it. Others, with a furrowed brow, weighing out grand words, philosophize about the Sacred Scriptures among groups of women. **O**thers—shameful to say—learn from women what they then teach to men; and as if that weren't enough, with a certain facility of words, or rather audacity, they explain to others what they do not understand themselves. I say nothing of those like myself who, if they happen to come to the Holy Scriptures after a background in secular literature, and have charmed the ears of the people with polished speech, think that whatever they say is the Law of God. Nor do they deign to know what the prophets or the apostles actually meant; instead, they adapt mismatched testimonies to suit their own meaning. As if it were a great thing—and not rather the most faulty style of speaking—to distort sentences and drag the reluctant Holy Scripture toward one's own will. 

As if we had not read the "Homer-Centos" or "Virgil-Centos!" <note>A "cento" was a poem made by patching together lines from famous authors to tell a completely different story. Jerome is arguing that people treat the Bible like a game of literary collage.</note> And as if we could not also call Virgil <note>original: "Maronem", referring to Publius Vergilius Maro.</note> a "Christian without Christ" because he wrote: *"Now the Virgin returns, the reign of Saturn returns; now a new progeny is sent down from high heaven."* <note>Virgil, *Eclogue* 4. This poem was often misinterpreted by early Christians as a prophecy of Christ's birth.</note> And the father speaking to the son: *"My son, my strength, my great power alone."* <note>Virgil, *Aeneid* 1.664. In the original, Venus speaks to Cupid; here, Jerome notes how people misapply it to God the Father and the Son.</note> **A**nd after the words of the Savior on the cross: *"Such things he offered as he spoke, and remained fixed."* <note>Virgil, *Aeneid* 10.821-822. Jerome is mocking the absurdity of using Virgil's description of a dying warrior to describe Christ's crucifixion.</note> These things are childish, similar to the games of street-performers: to teach what you do not know—or rather, to speak with some irritation—not even to know that you are ignorant.

<margin>
From the description until Egypt
</margin>

**G**<insert>Asas. m. vii.</insert>enesis is where one reads of the creation of the world, the beginning of the human race, the division of the earth, and the confusion of languages and nations, right up to the departure of the Hebrews from Egypt. **E**xodus is clear with its ten plagues, the Decalogue <note>The Ten Commandments.</note>, and mystic and divine precepts. **T**he book of **L**eviticus is at hand, in which every single sacrifice—indeed, almost every single syllable, and the garments of Aaron, and the entire Levitical order—breathes of heavenly sacraments. 

**D**o not the **N**umbers contain the mysteries of all arithmetic, the prophecy of Balaam, and the forty-two stations <note>The stopping points of the Israelites during the Exodus.</note> through the wilderness? **D**euteronomy, the "Second Law" and a prefiguration of the Gospel law—does it not contain the earlier things in such a way that they all become new from the old? **T**hus far Moses, thus far the Pentateuch <note>The first five books of the Bible.</note>, in which "five words" the Apostle <note>Paul, in 1 Corinthians 14:19.</note> glories that he wishes to speak in the church. 

**J**ob, that model of patience—what mysteries does he not embrace in his speech? **H**e begins in prose, glides into verse, and ends in pedestrian speech; he determines all the laws of logic through proposition, assumption, confirmation, and conclusion. Every single word in him is full of meaning. And to say nothing of the rest, he prophesies the resurrection of bodies so clearly that no one has written of it more plainly or carefully. *"I know,"* he says, *"that my Redeemer lives, and on the last day I shall rise from the earth: and again I shall be surrounded by my skin, and in my flesh I shall see God; whom I myself shall see, and my eyes shall behold, and not another. This hope is laid up in my bosom."* <note>Job 19:25-27.</note>

**I** come to **J**oshua <note>original: "ihesum naue", Jesus son of Nave/Nun.</note> who bears the "type" <note>A "type" is a person or event in the Old Testament seen as a symbolic foreshadowing of Christ.</note> of the Lord not only in his deeds, but even in his name. He crosses the Jordan, subverts the kingdoms of enemies, divides the land for the victorious people; and through individual cities, villages, mountains, rivers, torrents, and boundaries, he describes the spiritual kingdoms of the heavenly Jerusalem. **I**n the book of **J**udges, there are as many figures as there are leaders of the people. **R**uth the Moabite fulfills the prophecy of Isaiah, saying: *"Send forth the lamb, O Lord, the ruler of the earth, from the rock of the desert to the mount of the daughter of Zion."* <note>Isaiah 16:1.</note> **S**amuel shows the old law abolished in the death of Eli and the killing of Saul...

<summary>Jerome critiques unqualified people who treat Bible interpretation like a literary game and provides a concise summary of the spiritual significance of the books from Genesis to Samuel.</summary>
<keywords>Jerome, Paulinus of Nola, Bible interpretation, Centos, Virgil, Pentateuch, Genesis, Exodus, Leviticus, Numbers, Deuteronomy, Job, Joshua, Judges, Ruth, Samuel</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues Saint Jerome’s "Letter to Paulinus." Having previously critiqued those who misinterpret Scripture, Jerome now begins a rapid survey of the books of the Bible, explaining their spiritual and prophetic significance. He has just finished discussing the books of Samuel (1 and 2 Kings in the older numbering).</meta>

# . Letter .

...shows; furthermore, in Zadok and David he testifies to the sacred mysteries <note>original: "sacramenta"</note> of a new priesthood and a new empire. **M**alachim <note>Hebrew: "Kings." Jerome refers here to what we now call 1 and 2 Kings.</note>, that is the third and fourth book of Kings, describes the kingdom of Judah and the kingdom of Israel from the time of Solomon until Zedekiah, and from Jeroboam the son of Nebat until Hoshea, who was led away captive into Assyria. If you look at the history, the words are simple; but if you look at the hidden sense within the letter, the small numbers of the Church and the wars of the heretics against the Church are narrated.

**T**he Twelve Prophets <note>The "Minor Prophets," traditionally written on a single scroll.</note>, compressed into the narrow space of one volume, prefigure something far different than what they sound like according to the literal text. **H**osea frequently names Ephraim, Samaria, Joseph, and Jezreel; he speaks of a "wife of fornication" and "sons of fornication," and an adulteress shut in her husband's chamber, sitting for a long time as a widow in mourning clothes, waiting for her husband to return to her.

**J**oel, the son of Phatuel, describes the land of the twelve tribes being devastated by the caterpillar, the cankerworm, the locust, and the blight. He tells how, after the consumption of the former people, the Holy Spirit was to be poured out upon the servants and handmaids of God—that is, upon the names of the hundred and twenty believers <note>The number of disciples present at Pentecost in Acts 1:15.</note>—and how it would shine forth in the upper room of Zion. These hundred and twenty, rising gradually from one to fifteen, make up the number of the fifteen steps <note>Jerome links the number of believers to the fifteen "Songs of Ascents" (Psalms 120–134) traditionally sung on the steps of the Temple.</note> which are mystically contained in the Psalter.

**A**mos, a shepherd and a rustic, and a dresser of sycamore figs <note>original: "ruborum mora," berries of the brambles.</note>, cannot be explained in just a few words. For who could worthily describe the "three and four crimes" of Damascus, Gaza, Tyre, Edom, the sons of Ammon, and Moab? And in the seventh and eighth degree, the crimes of Judah and Israel? He speaks to the fat cows that are on the mountain of Samaria, and testifies that the greater and smaller house shall fall. He sees the creator of the locust, and the Lord standing upon a plastered or adamantine wall, and a hook of fruit <note>A reference to the vision of the basket of summer fruit in Amos 8.</note> drawing punishments toward sinners, and a famine in the land—not a famine of bread, nor a thirst for water, but of hearing the word of God.

<column-break/>

**O**badiah, whose name is interpreted as "Servant of the Lord," thunders against Edom; with a spiritual spear, he strikes the bloody and earthly brother who was always a rival to Jacob. 

**J**onah, the "most beautiful dove," prefigures the passion of the Lord by his own shipwreck. He calls the world to penance and, under the name of Nineveh, announces salvation to the nations. 

**M**icah of Moresheth, a co-heir of Christ, announces a prophecy to the "daughter of the thief" and describes a siege set against her, because she struck the jaw of the judge of Israel. 

**N**ahum, the "consoler of the world," rebukes the "city of blood" <note>Nineveh.</note>; and after its overthrow, he speaks: "Behold upon the mountains the feet of him who brings good news and announces peace!" 

**H**abakkuk, a strong and rigid wrestler, stands upon his guard-post and fixes his step upon the fortification so that he may contemplate Christ on the cross. He says: "His glory covered the heavens, and the earth is full of his praise; his splendor shall be as the light; horns are in his hands; there his strength was hidden." 

**Z**ephaniah, the lookout and observer of the secrets of the Lord, hears a cry from the Fish Gate, a wailing from the "Second District," and a crashing from the hills. He proclaims a howling to the inhabitants of the Mortar <note>original: "pile," a district in Jerusalem.</note>, because all the people of Canaan have fallen silent, and all those who were wrapped in silver have been dispersed. 

**H**aggai, festive and joyful, who sowed in tears so that he might reap in joy, builds the destroyed temple. He brings in God the Father speaking: "Yet once, a little while, and I will shake the heaven and the earth and the sea and the dry land; and I will move all nations; and the Desired of all nations shall come." 

**Z**echariah, "mindful of his Lord," is manifold in his prophecy. He sees Jesus <note>original: "Iesum." Jerome refers to Joshua the High Priest, but uses the name "Jesus" to emphasize the prophetic "type" of Christ.</note> clothed in filthy garments, a lamp of seven eyes, and a golden candlestick with as many lamps as eyes, and two olive trees to the left of the lamp...

<summary>Jerome provides a theological and symbolic survey of the books of Kings and the twelve Minor Prophets, explaining how their historical narratives and visions prefigure Christ and the Church.</summary>
<keywords>Minor Prophets, Hosea, Joel, Amos, Obadiah, Jonah, Micah, Nahum, Habakkuk, Zephaniah, Haggai, Zechariah, typology, biblical interpretation, Old Testament</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues Saint Jerome’s "Letter to Paulinus." Jerome is currently providing a "tour" of the books of the Bible, explaining the spiritual significance of each. He is concluding his overview of the Prophets and the Old Testament before transitioning into the New Testament. The text emphasizes that the Bible contains deep, hidden mysteries ("marrow") beneath its literal surface ("bark").</meta>

# Letter

...on the right hand: so that after the red, dappled, black, and white horses, and the scattered chariots of Ephraim and the horse of Jerusalem, he may prophesy of the poor king, and preach of a king sitting upon a foal, the offspring of a beast of burden <note>Jerome is describing the visions of Zechariah, specifically the horses in Zechariah 1 and 6, and the entry into Jerusalem in Zechariah 9:9.</note>. **M**alachi, openly and at the end of all the prophets, speaks of the rejection of Israel and the calling of the nations: "I have no pleasure in you, says the Lord of hosts, and I will not receive an offering from your hand." <note>original: "non est michi voluntas in vobis dicit dn̄s exercitui: ⁊ munꝰ nō suscipiam de manu vestra."</note> "For from the rising of the sun even to its setting, my name is great among the nations; and in every place sacrifice is made and a pure offering is offered to my name."

**I**saiah, Jeremiah, Ezekiel, and Daniel: who can either understand or explain them? **T**he first of these seems to me to compose not a prophecy, but a Gospel. **T**he second describes a rod of an almond tree and a boiling pot facing away from the north, and a leopard stripped of its colors <note>original: "pardum spoliatū suis coloribus." This likely refers to the variety of styles in Lamentations.</note>; and he weaves a fourfold alphabet in various meters. **T**he third <note>Ezekiel</note> has the beginnings and the end involved in such great obscurities that, among the Hebrews, those parts—along with the beginning of Genesis—are not read before the age of thirty. **T**he fourth <note>Daniel</note>, who is also the last among the four prophets, is conscious of the times and a lover of world history; in clear speech he proclaims the precious stone cut from the mountain without hands, which subverts all kingdoms.

**D**avid, our Simonides, Pindar, and Alcaeus, our Horace, Catullus, and Serenus, sounds out Christ with his lyre; and on the ten-stringed psaltery, he awakens the one rising from the dead. **S**olomon, the "Peaceable One" and the "Beloved of the Lord," <note>Jerome explains the meanings of the names Solomon and Jedidiah.</note> corrects nature, teaches it, joins the Church and Christ, and sings a sweet wedding song <term>epithalamium: a song or poem celebrating a marriage</term> for their holy nuptials. **H**ester <note>Esther</note>, as a type of the Church, delivers the people from danger; and after she slays Haman—whose name means "iniquity"—she passes down a share of the feast and a day of

<column-break/>

celebration to posterity. **T**he book of Chronicles <note>original: "Paralipomenon," literally "things omitted."</note>, an epitome of the Old Testament record, is of such great importance that if anyone wishes to claim knowledge of the Scriptures without it, he only mocks himself. Indeed, through every individual name and the connections of words, histories touched upon in the Books of Kings are explained, and innumerable questions of the Gospel are unfolded. **E**zra and Nehemiah—that is, the "Helper" and the "Consoler" from the Lord—are bound together in one volume; they restore the Temple and build up the walls of the city. That whole crowd of people returning to their fatherland, the census of the priests, the Levites, the Israelites, and the proselytes, and the work on the walls and towers divided among individual families, display one thing on the bark <note>original: "in cortice," meaning the literal surface of the text.</note> but retain another in the marrow <note>original: "in medulla," meaning the deep spiritual sense.</note>.

**Y**ou see me, seized by love for the Scriptures, having exceeded the limit of a letter; and yet I have not fulfilled what I wished. **W**e have heard only what we ought to know and what we ought to desire, so that we too may be able to say: "My soul has longed to desire your justifications at all times." <note>A quote from Psalm 119:20.</note> **H**owever, that Socratic saying is fulfilled in us: "**T**his much only I know: that I know nothing."

**I** shall also briefly touch upon the New Testament. **M**atthew, Mark, Luke, and John are the chariot of the Lord and the true Cherubim—which means "fullness of knowledge." They are covered with eyes throughout their whole body; sparks flash forth, lightning runs to and fro, they have straight feet pointing upward, winged backs, and they fly everywhere. They hold fast to one another and are intertwined; and like a wheel within a wheel, they roll along and proceed wherever the breath of the Holy Spirit leads them. **P**aul the Apostle writes to seven churches; for the eighth, to the Hebrews, is placed outside the number by many. **H**e instructs Timothy and Titus, and intercedes with Philemon for a runaway slave; on which subject I think it better to remain silent than to write too little. **T**he Acts of the Apostles seem to offer a simple...

<summary>Jerome provides a rapid symbolic and literal summary of the Old Testament books (Major Prophets, Psalms, Solomon, Esther, Chronicles, Ezra) and begins an introduction to the New Testament (Gospels, Paul's Epistles).</summary>
<keywords>Major Prophets, Book of Chronicles, Ezra and Nehemiah, Four Gospels, Cherubim, Saint Paul's Epistles, Biblical Interpretation, spiritual sense vs literal sense</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes Saint Jerome’s "Letter to Paulinus" (Letter 53), which serves as a general introduction to the study of the Bible. After finishing his survey of the New Testament—specifically the Epistles and Revelation—Jerome encourages Paulinus to live a life of poverty and devotion. The page then transitions into Jerome’s "Prologue to the Pentateuch," addressed to his friend Desiderius, where he defends his new translation of the first five books of the Bible from Hebrew into Latin.</meta>

# Letter

...to sound like history and to guide the infancy of the nascent Church <note>Jerome is referring here to the Book of Acts.</note>. But should I mention that their author is Luke the physician, whose praise is in the Gospel? We notice that all his words are likewise medicine for a languishing soul. James, Peter, John, and Jude issued seven epistles as mystical as they are succinct; they are both short and long: short in words, but long in their meanings <note>original: "longas in sententijs"</note>, so that there is rarely anyone who does not stumble in the reading of them. The Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation</note> of John has as many mysteries <note>original: "sacramenta"</note> as it has words. Even this is saying too little, and all praise falls short of the book’s merit. Within each individual word, multiple layers of understanding lie hidden.

I pray you, dearest brother, to live among these things, to meditate upon them, to know nothing else, and to seek nothing else. Does this not seem to you to be a dwelling-place of the celestial kingdom already here on earth? I do not want you to be offended in the Holy Scriptures by the simplicity and, as it were, the lowliness of the words, which—either through the fault of translators or by deliberate design—were brought forth in such a way as to more easily instruct a rural congregation. Thus, in one and the same sentence, the learned man might feel one way, and the unlearned another. I am not so bold or dull as to promise that I know these things myself, or that I can pluck the fruits on earth whose roots are fixed in heaven. But I confess that I desire to; I place myself ahead of those who remain idle. In coming to you as a teacher, I promise myself as a companion. To him who asks, it is given; to him who knocks, it is opened; he who seeks, finds. Let us learn on earth those things whose knowledge will persist with us in heaven. I will receive you with open arms, and—so that I might pour forth something inaptly and with the pomposity <note>original: "tumiditate," meaning "swelling" or "inflation"</note> of a Hermagoras <note>Hermagoras of Temnos was a famous Greek rhetorician; Jerome is mocking overly-elaborate academic styles.</note>—I will strive to know whatever you seek along with you. Chapter 8.

<margin><unclear>i. f.</unclear></margin>
**Y**ou have here your most beloved brother, Eusebius <note>Likely Eusebius of Cremona, a close associate of Jerome.</note>, who has doubled for me the grace of your letters by reporting the integrity of your character, your contempt for the world, the faithfulness of your friendship, and your love for Christ. For even without him, your letter itself displayed your wisdom and the beauty of your eloquence. 

<column-break/>

Hasten, I pray you, and rather cut the rope of your little boat stuck in the surf than untie it. No one who is about to renounce the world can profitably sell the things he has despised in order to sell them. Whatever you take from your own property for your expenses, count it as profit. It is an ancient saying: "The miser lacks as much what he has as what he does not have." To the believer, the whole world is a wealth of riches. The infidel, however, lacks even a penny <note>original: "obulo," a small coin.</note>. Let us live as if we have nothing, yet possess everything. Food and clothing are the riches of Christians. If you have your property in your power, sell it; if you do not have it, throw it away. To him who takes your tunic, your cloak also must be left behind. <margin>"</margin>"Unless, of course, you—always procrastinating and dragging things out from day to day—sell your little possessions so cautiously and step-by-step that Christ has nothing from which to feed his poor. He has given everything to God who has offered himself. The Apostles left only their ship and their nets; the widow puts two small coins into the treasury <note>original: "gazophilatiū"</note>, and she is preferred over the riches of heaven. He easily despises all things who always thinks that he is about to die.

### Here begins the prologue to the Pentateuch of Moses.

**I** have received the long-desired letters of my Desiderius <note>Jerome addresses this prologue to his friend Desiderius.</note>, who, by a certain omen of things to come, bears a name as strong as Daniel’s <note>Jerome plays on the name Desiderius (meaning "desire") and the biblical Daniel, whom he calls a "man of desires" (Daniel 9:23).</note>. He implores me to deliver the Pentateuch <note>The first five books of the Bible (Genesis, Exodus, Leviticus, Numbers, Deuteronomy).</note>, translated from the Hebrew speech into the Latin language, for the ears of our people. This is certainly a perilous work, exposed to the barkings of my detractors, who assert that I am forging new things to replace the old as an insult to the Seventy Interpreters <note>The Septuagint, the traditional Greek translation of the Old Testament.</note>. They test my talent as if it were wine—even though I have very often testified that I offer what I can as a humble portion to the tabernacle of God, and that the riches of one person are not defiled by the poverty of others. So that I might dare this, the zeal of Origen <note>A famous, though controversial, 3rd-century Christian scholar who studied the various versions of the Bible.</note>...

<summary>This page contains the conclusion of Jerome's famous letter to Paulinus, urging a life of Christian poverty and scriptural study, followed by the beginning of his defense of translating the Pentateuch directly from Hebrew.</summary>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues Saint Jerome’s "Prologue to the Pentateuch," addressed to his friend Desiderius. Jerome is defending his decision to translate the Old Testament directly from the original Hebrew (the "Hebrew Verity") rather than relying solely on the Greek Septuagint. He addresses the discrepancies between the Hebrew text and the Greek version used by the early Church.</meta>

# Prologue

...provoked him: who mixed the translation of Theodotion <note>A 2nd-century Jewish convert who translated the Hebrew Bible into Greek</note> into the ancient edition, distinguishing the whole work with the asterisk <note>original: "asterisco"; a star symbol used by Jerome to mark words found in the Hebrew but missing in the Greek</note> and the obelisk <note>original: "uerũ" / "obelo"; a horizontal line or dagger symbol used to mark words present in the Greek but missing in the Hebrew</note>. He does this while either making those things shine which were previously missing, or he slashes and stabs through all that is superfluous. This is especially true regarding the passages that the authority of the Evangelists and Apostles has proclaimed. In these, we read many things from the Old Testament which are not found in our manuscripts—such as: "Out of Egypt I called my son" <note>Hosea 11:1</note>; and "Because he shall be called a Nazarene" <note>Matthew 2:23</note>; and "They shall look upon him whom they have pierced" <note>Zechariah 12:10</note>; and "Rivers of living water shall flow from his belly" <note>John 7:38</note>; and "What no eye has seen, nor ear heard, nor has it entered into the heart of man, which things God has prepared for those who love him" <note>1 Corinthians 2:9</note>; and many other things which require a proper treatise. We ask them, therefore: where are these things written? And when they cannot say, I shall bring them forth from the Hebrew books.

<margin>867</margin>
The first testimony is in Hosea. The second in Isaiah. The third in Zechariah. The fourth in Proverbs. The fifth equally in Isaiah—which many, in their ignorance, read as the ravings of the apocrypha <note>Non-canonical or "hidden" books not accepted as scripture</note>, and prefer Spanish nonsense <note>original: "hiberas nenias"; a derogatory reference to the mystical or heretical writings of the Priscillianists in Spain</note> over the authentic books. It is not my place to explain the causes of this error.

<margin>Regarding</margin>
The Jews say this was done with prudent counsel: lest Ptolemy <note>Ptolemy II Philadelphus, the Greek King of Egypt who commissioned the Septuagint translation</note>, being a worshiper of only one God, might perceive a double divinity among the Hebrews. They did this especially because it seemed to fall into the dogma of Plato <note>A reference to Platonic ideas of a primary God and a secondary creator or "demiurge"</note>. Finally, wherever the Scripture testifies to something sacred concerning the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit, they either interpreted it differently or remained silent altogether, so that they might both satisfy the King and not make public the secret of the faith. And I do not know who the first author was who, by his own lie, constructed the seventy cells at Alexandria <note>A legend claimed the 70 translators were isolated in separate cells yet miraculously produced identical Greek texts</note>, in which they wrote the same things although divided; since Aristeas, a bodyguard of the same Ptolemy, and Josephus, not long after that time, recorded no such thing. Instead...

<column-break/>

...they write that they conferred together, gathered in a single hall <note>original: "basilica"</note>, and did not "prophesy." For it is one thing to be a prophet <note>original: "uatem"</note>, and another to be an interpreter. In the former, the Spirit predicts things to come; in the latter, learning and a wealth of words translate what is understood. Unless, perhaps, we must think that Cicero <note>original: "tullius"</note> translated Xenophon's *Economics*, Plato's *Protagoras* <note>The text reads "pitagorã" (Pythagoras), likely a scribal error for Protagoras</note>, and Demosthenes' *On the Crown* <note>original: "prothelisontem"</note> while inspired by a rhetorical spirit. Otherwise, the Holy Spirit guides testimonies from the same books one way through the seventy interpreters, and another way through the Apostles—implying that what the former kept silent, the latter falsely claimed was written. What then? Do we condemn the ancients? Not at all! But after the studies of those who came before us, we labor as much as we can in the house of the Lord. They interpreted before the coming of Christ, and what they did not know, they expressed in doubtful sentences. We write after his passion—not so much prophecy as history. For things heard are narrated one way, and things seen another. Insofar as we understand better, we also express it better. Listen then, rival; listen, detractor! I do not condemn, I do not rebuke the Seventy; but I confidently prefer the Apostles to all of them. Through the mouths of the latter, Christ speaks to me—those whom I read are placed among the spiritual gifts <note>original: "carismata"</note> before the prophets, and in which the interpreters hold almost the final rank. Why are you twisted with envy? Why do you incite the minds of the unlearned against me? If anywhere in the translation I seem to you to err, ask the Hebrews; consult the masters of various cities. What their books have concerning Christ, yours do not. It is another matter if they later rejected the testimonies used against them by the Apostles; and [do you really think] that Latin manuscripts are more correct than Greek, and Greek more correct than Hebrew? Truly, these things are against the envious. Now I beseech you, dearest Desiderius, that since you have made me undertake such a great work—to take my beginning from Genesis—help me with your prayers, so that I may be able to translate these books into the Latin tongue with the same Spirit by which they were written.

<summary>Saint Jerome defends his new Latin translation of the Old Testament from Hebrew, arguing that the earlier Greek Septuagint obscured certain truths about the Trinity and that the Apostles' own quotes prove the Hebrew original is the most reliable source.</summary>
<keywords>Jerome, Septuagint, Hebrew Verity, Ptolemy, translation theory, biblical prophecy, Desiderius, Aristeas, Josephus</keywords>

<meta>Following the conclusion of Saint Jerome’s prologue, where he defended his "Hebrew Verity" translation, this page marks the actual beginning of the Biblical text. The transition moves from the translator's scholarly defense to the sacred narrative of creation itself.</meta>

# Genesis

<meta>This page features a highly ornate floral border with gold leaf and a large, decorated initial "I" at the start of the text. The script is a formal Gothic textualis, and the opening line is rubricated in red ink.</meta>

### The beginning of the book of Bereshit, which we call Genesis.
<note>original: "bresith"; this is the Hebrew word *Bereshit*, meaning "In the beginning," which serves as the title of the book in the Hebrew Bible.</note>

**I**n the beginning God created heaven <insert>chapter one</insert> and the earth. And the earth was empty and void: and darkness was upon the face of the abyss: and the spirit of God was moved over the waters. And God said: Let there be light. And light was made. And God saw the light, that it was good: and he divided the light from the darkness. And he called the light Day, and the darkness Night. And there was evening and morning, one day.

And God also said: Let there be a firmament <term>firmament</term> <note>From the Latin *firmamentum*, referring to the vast solid dome of the sky that ancient peoples believed separated the waters above the heavens from the waters on the earth.</note> in the midst of the waters: and let it divide the waters from the waters. And God made the firmament: and he divided the waters that were under the firmament from those that were above the firmament: and it was so. And God called the firmament Heaven; and there was evening and morning, the second day.

And God truly said: Let the waters that are under the heaven be gathered together into one place: and let the dry land appear. And it was so. And God called the dry land Earth: and the gathering together of the waters he called Seas. And God saw that it was good. And he said: Let the earth bring forth green herbs, such as may seed, and fruit trees yielding fruit after its kind, whose seed may be in itself upon the earth. And it was so. And the earth brought forth green herbs, and such as yield seed according to its kind: and the tree yielding fruit, having each one seed according to its species. And God saw that it was good: and there was evening and morning, the third day.

And God said: Let there be lights in the firmament of heaven, and let them divide the day and the night: and let them be for signs, and for seasons, and for days and years: that they may shine in the firmament of heaven and illuminate the earth. And it was so. And God made two great lights: a greater light to rule the day, and a lesser light to rule the night: and also the stars. And he set them in the firmament of heaven to shine upon the earth: and to rule the day and the night: and to divide the light and the darkness. And God saw that it was good: and there was evening and morning, the fourth day.

God also said: Let the waters bring forth the creeping creature having a living soul, and the winged creature over the earth, under the firmament of heaven. And God created the great sea monsters <note>original: "cete grandia"; often translated as "great whales," this refers to any massive sea creature.</note>, and every living and moving soul which the waters produced according to their species, and every winged creature according to its kind. And God saw that it was good: and he blessed them, saying: Increase and multiply, and fill the waters of the sea: and let the birds be multiplied upon the earth. And there was evening and morning, the fifth day.

And God also said: Let the earth bring forth the living soul in its kind: cattle and creeping things, and beasts of the earth according to their species. It was so. And God made the beasts of the earth according to their species: cattle, and every creeping thing of the earth in its kind. And God saw that it was good: and he said: Let us make man to our image and likeness: and let him have dominion over the fishes of the sea, and the fowls of the air, and the beasts of the whole earth, and every creeping thing that moveth upon the earth. And God created man to his own image and likeness: to the image of God he created him: male and female he created them. And God blessed them, and said: Increase and multiply, and fill the earth, and subdue it, and rule over the fishes of the sea, and the fowls of the air, and all living creatures that move upon the earth. And God said: Behold I have given you every herb bearing seed upon the earth, and all trees which have in themselves seed of their own kind: to be food for you, and for all the animals of the earth, and for every fowl of the air, and for all that move upon the earth, and in which there is a living soul, that they may have to eat. And it was so. And God saw all the things that he had made: and they were very good.

<summary>This page contains the opening chapter of Genesis, detailing the biblical account of the six days of Creation. It establishes the origin of the world, from the separation of light and darkness to the creation of humanity in the divine image.</summary>

# . Genesis .

And there was evening and morning, the sixth day. **. Chapter 2 .**
So the heavens and the earth were finished, and all the decoration <note>original: "ornatus"; often translated as "host" or "array," referring to the stars and all living things.</note> of them. And on the seventh day God ended his work which he had made: and he rested on the seventh day from all his work which he had done. And he blessed the seventh day and sanctified it: because in it he had ceased from all his work which God created and made. These are the generations of the heaven and the earth when they were created, in the day that the Lord God made the heaven and the earth: and every shrub of the field before it sprang up in the earth: and every herb of the region before it germinated. For the Lord God had not rained upon the earth: and there was no man to work the earth. But a spring rose up from the earth, watering the entire surface of the earth.

Therefore the Lord God formed man from the dust <term>slime</term> <note>Latin "limo"; refers to the moist earth or clay, emphasizing the humble material of man's physical origin.</note> of the earth: and breathed into his face the breath of life: and man became a living soul. Now the Lord God had planted a paradise of pleasure <note>original: "paradisum voluptatis"; this is the Vulgate's way of describing the Garden of Eden.</note> from the beginning: in which he placed the man whom he had formed. And the Lord God produced from the ground every tree beautiful to behold and sweet to eat: the tree of life also in the midst of paradise: and the tree of the knowledge of good and evil. And a river went out from the place of pleasure to water paradise: which from there is divided into four headwaters. The name of one is Pishon. It is that which circles the entire 
<margin>men</margin> 
land of Havilah, where gold is born: and the gold of that land is the best. There also is found bdellium <note>An aromatic resin similar to myrrh.</note> and the onyx stone. And the name of the second river is Gihon. It is that which circles the entire land of Ethiopia. Truly the name of the third river is Tigris. It goes toward the Assyrians. And the fourth river is the Euphrates.

The Lord God therefore took the man and placed him in the paradise of pleasure, that he might work it and keep it: and he commanded him, saying: From every tree of paradise you may eat: but from the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, do not eat. For in whatever day you eat from it, you shall die the death. And the Lord God said: It is not good for man to be alone: let us make him a helper like unto himself. Therefore, the Lord God having formed from the ground all the animals of the earth and all the birds of the air, he brought them to Adam to see what he would call them. For whatever Adam called any living soul, that is its name. And Adam called by their names all the animals and all the birds of the air and all the beasts of the earth. But for Adam there was not found a helper like himself.

And so the Lord God sent a deep sleep upon Adam. And when he was asleep, he took one of his ribs: and filled up flesh for it. And the Lord God built the rib which he had taken from Adam into a woman: and brought her to Adam. And Adam said: This is now bone of my bones, and flesh of my flesh. She shall be called woman <note>original: "virago"; in Latin, this is a play on "vir" (man). Adam is naming her based on her origin from man.</note>, because she was taken from man. Wherefore a man shall leave his father and mother and shall cleave to his wife: and they shall be two in one flesh. And they were both naked, namely Adam and his wife: and they were not ashamed.

But the serpent was craftier **. Chapter 3 .** than all the animals of the earth which the Lord God had made. And he said to the woman: Why has God commanded you that you should not eat of every tree of paradise? To whom the woman answered: Of the fruit of the trees that are in paradise we do eat: but of the fruit of the tree which is in the midst of paradise, God has commanded us that we should not eat, and that we should not touch it, lest perhaps we die. And the serpent said to the woman: You shall by no means die the death. For God knows that in whatever day you eat of it, your eyes shall be opened: and you shall be as gods, knowing good and evil. Therefore the woman saw that the tree was good for eating, and beautiful to the eyes, and delightful to behold: and she took of the fruit thereof and ate: and gave...

# . Genesis .

...and she gave it to her husband. And he ate: and the eyes of both were opened. And when they knew that they were naked: they sewed fig leaves together and made themselves aprons <note>original: "perizomata"; referring to simple loincloths or coverings.</note>. And when they heard the voice of the Lord God walking in paradise in the afternoon breeze: Adam and his wife hid themselves from the face of the Lord God in the midst of the trees of paradise. And the Lord God called Adam: and said to him, “Where are you?” And he said, “I heard your voice, Lord, in paradise, and I was afraid because I was naked, and I hid myself.” To whom the Lord said, “Who indeed told you that you were naked, unless it is that you have eaten from the tree of which I commanded you that you should not eat?” **A**nd Adam said, “The woman whom you gave me as a companion gave to me from the tree, and I ate.” **A**nd the Lord God said to the woman, “Why have you done this?” She answered, “The **S**erpent deceived me, and I ate.” **A**nd the Lord said to the serpent, “Because you have done this, you are cursed among all living things and beasts of the earth. Upon your breast shall you crawl, and earth shall you eat all the days of your life. I will put enmities between you and the woman, and your seed and her seed. She shall crush your head <note>original: "Ipsa conteret caput tuum"; the Latin Vulgate uses the feminine "she," which later Christian tradition often associated with the Virgin Mary, while other versions use "he" or "it."</note>, and you shall lie in wait for her heel.” **T**o the woman also he said, “I will multiply your hardships and your conceptions. In sorrow you shall bring forth children, and you shall be under the power of a husband, and he shall rule over you.” **B**ut to Adam he said, “Because you have listened to the voice of your wife, and have eaten of the tree from which I commanded you that you should not eat: cursed is the earth in your work. In labors you shall eat from it all the days of your life. Thorns and thistles shall it bring forth to you, and you shall eat the herbs of the earth. In the sweat of your face you shall eat your bread until you return to the earth from which you were taken: for dust you are, and into dust you shall return.” And Adam called the name of his wife Eve: because she was the mother of all the living. **T**he Lord God also made for Adam and his wife 

coats of skins, and clothed them. And he said, “**B**ehold, Adam has become like one of us, knowing good and evil. Now, therefore, let him not perhaps put forth his hand and take also from the tree of life, and eat, and live forever.” **T**he Lord God sent him out of the paradise of pleasure <note>original: "paradisum voluptatis"; the term used in the Vulgate for the Garden of Eden.</note> so that he might work the earth from which he was taken. And he cast out Adam, and placed before the paradise of pleasure Cherubim and a flaming sword, turning every way, to guard the way to the tree of life. **Chapter 4.**

<margin>cain</margin>
**A**dam truly knew Eve his wife: who conceived and gave birth to Cain, saying, “I have possessed a man through the Lord.” And again she gave birth to his brother Abel. Now Abel was a shepherd of sheep, and Cain a farmer. **A**nd it happened after many days that Cain offered gifts to the Lord from the fruits of the earth. **A**bel also offered from the firstborn of his flock and from their fat. **A**nd the Lord looked with favor upon Abel and his gifts, but to Cain and his gifts he did not look with favor. And Cain was vehemently angry, and his countenance fell. **A**nd the Lord said to him, “Why are you angry? And why is your face fallen? If you do well, will you not receive? But if you do ill, your sin shall be present at the very door. But the desire for it shall be under you, and you shall rule over it.” **A**nd Cain said to his brother, 
<margin>abel</margin>
“Let us go outside.” <note>original: "Egrediamur foras"; this phrase is found in the Vulgate and the Septuagint but is missing from the traditional Hebrew Masoretic text.</note> And when they were in the field, Cain rose up against his brother Abel and killed him. **A**nd the Lord said to Cain, “Where is Abel your brother?” Who answered, “I do not know. Am I my brother’s keeper?” **A**nd he said to him, “What have you done? The **v**oice of your brother’s blood cries out to me from the earth. **N**ow therefore you shall be cursed upon the earth, which has opened its mouth and received the blood of your brother from your hand. When you work the earth, it shall not give its fruits to you. A wanderer and a fugitive you shall be upon the earth.” **A**nd Cain said to the Lord, “My iniquity is greater than...

# Genesis

...that I may deserve forgiveness. Behold, you cast me out today from the face of the earth, and from your face I shall be hidden; and I shall be a vagabond and a fugitive on the earth. Everyone, therefore, who finds me will kill me. And the Lord said to him: "By no means shall it be so; but everyone who kills Cain shall be punished sevenfold." And the Lord set a mark <note>original: "signum"; often referred to as the 'Mark of Cain,' a divine sign intended to protect him from extrajudicial killing rather than a badge of shame.</note> upon Cain, so that no one who found him would kill him. 

And Cain, going out from the face of the Lord, dwelt as a fugitive in the land to the east of Eden. And Cain knew his wife, who conceived and gave birth to Enoch. And he built a city, and called its name after the name of his son, Enoch. Furthermore, Enoch begot Irad, and Irad begot Mehujael, and Mehujael begot Methushael, and Methushael begot Lamech. Who took two wives: the name of one was Adah, and the name of the other was Zillah. And Adah gave birth to Jabal <note>The OCR reads "iahel," likely a scribal variant or transcription of "Iabel."</note>, who was the father of those dwelling in tents and of shepherds. And his brother's name was Jubal; he was the father of those playing the lyre and the organ <note>original: "organo"; in a medieval context, this refers generally to wind instruments or pipes.</note>. Zillah also gave birth to Tubal-cain, who was a hammerer and a smith in all works of bronze and iron. Moreover, the sister of Tubal-cain was Naamah.
<margin><unclear>psalm?</unclear></margin>
And Lamech said to his wives, Adah and Zillah: "Hear my voice, wives of Lamech; pay heed to my speech. For I have killed a man to my wounding, and a youth to my bruising. Sevenfold vengeance shall be given for Cain, but for Lamech seventy times seven." <note>A poetic boast by Lamech, suggesting that if Cain is protected by a sevenfold curse, Lamech's own vengeance is far greater.</note>

Adam also knew his wife again, and she gave birth to a son, and called his name Seth, saying: "God has given me another seed in place of Abel, whom Cain killed." But to Seth also a son was born, whom he called Enos; this man began to call upon the name of the Lord.

### Chapter V.

**T**his is the book of the generation of Adam. In the day that God created man, he made him in the likeness of God. He created them male and female, and blessed them; and he called their name Adam <note>The Hebrew name "Adam" is also the general word for "Humankind."</note> in the day in which

<column-break/>

they were created. And Adam lived one hundred and thirty years, and begot a son in his own image and likeness, and called his name Seth. And the days of Adam, after he begot Seth, were eight hundred years; and he begot sons and daughters. And all the time that Adam lived was nine hundred and thirty years, and he died.

Seth also lived one hundred and five years, and begot Enos. And Seth lived, after he begot Enos, eight hundred and seven years, and begot sons and daughters. And all the days of Seth were nine hundred and twelve years, and he died. 

Enos lived ninety years, and begot Cainan. After whose birth he lived eight hundred and fifteen years, and begot sons and daughters. And all the days of Enos were nine hundred and five years, and he died. 

Cainan also lived seventy years, and begot Mahalalel. And Cainan lived, after he begot Mahalalel, eight hundred and forty years, and begot sons and daughters. And all the days of Cainan were nine hundred and ten years, and he died. 

Mahalalel lived sixty-five years, and begot Jared. And Mahalalel lived, after he begot Jared, eight hundred and thirty years, and begot sons and daughters. And all the days of Mahalalel were eight hundred and ninety-five years, and he died. 

<margin>4</margin> Jared lived one hundred and sixty-two years, and begot Enoch. And Jared lived, after he begot Enoch, eight hundred years, and begot sons and daughters. And all the days of Jared were nine hundred and sixty-two years, and he died. 

Furthermore, Enoch lived sixty-five years, and begot Methuselah. And Enoch walked with God. And Enoch lived, after he begot Methuselah, three hundred years, and begot sons and daughters. And all the days of Enoch were three hundred and sixty-five years. <note>The text notably omits "and he died" for Enoch, following the tradition that he was taken by God.</note>

# Genesis

And he walked with God and was seen no more, because God took him. **Methuselah also lived one hundred eighty-seven years, and he begot Lamech.** And Methuselah lived after he begot Lamech seven hundred eighty-two years, and he begot sons and daughters. And all the days of Methuselah were nine hundred sixty-nine years, and he died. Moreover, Lamech lived one hundred eighty-two years and begot a son; and he called his name Noah, saying: "This one shall comfort us from the works and labors of our hands in the earth, which the Lord has cursed." **And Lamech lived <note>original: "Dixitq;"; the scribe likely made a mistake here, writing "And Lamech said" instead of "Vixitque" (And Lamech lived).</note> after he begot Noah five hundred ninety-five years, and he begot sons and daughters.** And all the days of Lamech were seven hundred seventy-seven years, and he died. Noah, truly, when he was five hundred years old, begot Shem, Ham, and Japheth.

## Chapter VI

**And** when men began to be multiplied upon the earth and had procreated daughters, the sons of God, seeing the daughters of men that they were beautiful, took for themselves wives from all whom they had chosen. And God said: "My spirit shall not remain in man forever, because he is flesh; and his days shall be one hundred twenty years." Moreover, giants <note>original: "Gigantes"; referring to the Nephilim mentioned in Hebrew tradition.</note> were upon the earth in those days. For after the sons of God went in to the daughters of men and they bore children, these are the mighty ones of old, men of renown. Moreover, God, seeing that the malice of men was great on the earth, and that every thought of the heart was intent upon evil at all times, it repented Him <note>original: "penituit eum"; a theological term expressing God’s sorrow or change of heart regarding humanity's direction.</note> that He had made man on the earth. And providing for the future and touched with inward grief of heart, He said: "I will blot out man, whom I have created, from the face of the earth; from man even to the animals, from the creeping thing even to the birds of the air. For it repents me that I have made them." Noah, however, found grace before the Lord. These are the generations of Noah. Noah was a 

<column-break/>

just and perfect man in his generations; he walked with God. And he begot three sons: Shem, Ham, and Japheth. Moreover, the earth was corrupted before the Lord and was filled with iniquity. And when God had seen the earth to be corrupted (for indeed all flesh had corrupted its way upon the earth), He said to Noah: "The end of all flesh is come before me. The earth is filled with iniquity from their face, and I will destroy them with the earth. Make for yourself an ark of planed wood <note>original: "lignis leuigatis"; the Vulgate's interpretation of the 'gopher wood' mentioned in Hebrew.</note>. You shall make little rooms in the ark, and you shall smear it with pitch within and without. And thus shall you make it: the length of the ark shall be three hundred cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length based on the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger, roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters.</note>, its width fifty cubits, and its height thirty cubits. You shall make a window in the ark, and you shall finish its summit in a cubit. Moreover, you shall place the door of the ark in its side; you shall make in it lower, middle, and third stories. Behold, I will bring the waters of a flood upon the earth, so that I may kill all flesh in which is the spirit of life under heaven; and all things that are on the earth shall be consumed. and I will establish my covenant with you; and you shall enter the ark, you and your sons, your wife, and the wives of your sons with you. And of all living things of all flesh, you shall bring pairs into the ark, so that they may live with you, of the male sex and the female. Of the birds according to their kind, and of the cattle in their kind, and of every creeping thing of the earth according to its kind; pairs of all shall enter with you, so that they may be able to live. You shall therefore take with you of all foods which can be eaten, and you shall gather them with you; and they shall be for food both for you and for them." Noah, therefore, did all things which God had commanded him.

years after he fathered Reu: and he fathered sons and daughters. **M**oreover, Reu lived thirty-two years and fathered Serug <note>original: "saruch"</note>. **A**nd Reu lived two hundred and seven years after he fathered Serug: and he fathered sons and daughters. **M**oreover, Serug lived thirty years and fathered Nahor. **A**nd Serug lived two hundred and thirty years after he fathered Nahor: and he fathered sons and daughters. **M**oreover, Nahor lived twenty-nine years and fathered Terah <note>original: "thare"</note>. **N**ahor lived one hundred and nineteen years after he fathered Terah: and he fathered sons and daughters. **A**nd Terah lived seventy years and fathered Abram, Nahor, and Haran <note>original: "aram"</note>. **T**hese are the generations of Terah. **T**erah fathered Abram, Nahor, and Haran. **F**urthermore, Haran fathered Lot. **A**nd Haran died before his father Terah in the land of his birth, in Ur of the Chaldeans. **A**nd Nahor and Abram took wives. **T**he name of Abram's wife was Sarai: and the name of Nahor's wife was Milcah, the daughter of Haran, the father of Milcah and the father of Iscah. **B**ut Sarai was barren, nor did she have children. **T**herefore, Terah took his son Abram and his grandson Lot, the son of Haran, and his daughter-in-law Sarai, the wife of his son Abram, and led them out from Ur of the Chaldeans to go into the land of Canaan. **A**nd they came as far as Haran <note>The city of Haran in Upper Mesopotamia, not to be confused with the person Haran.</note> and lived there. **A**nd the days of Terah were two hundred and five years, and he died in Haran.

### Chapter XIII

**A**bram therefore ascended out of Egypt, he and his wife and all that he had, and Lot with him, toward the southern region. Now he was very rich in the possession of gold and silver. And he returned by the way he had come, from the south to Bethel, as far as the place where he had previously fixed his tent between Bethel and Ai: in the place of the altar which he had made before; and there he called upon the name of the Lord. But Lot also, who was with Abram, had flocks of sheep and herds and tents; nor was the land able to hold them so that they might dwell together. For their substance was indeed great, and they could not live in common. Wherefore also a strife arose between the shepherds of the flocks of Abram and of Lot. At that time the Canaanite and the Perizzite <note>An ancient people living in the land of Canaan.</note> lived in that land. Abram said therefore to Lot: "Let there be no quarrel, I pray you, between me and you, and between my shepherds and your shepherds. For we are brothers. Behold, the whole land is before you; depart from me, I pray you. If you go to the left, I will keep to the right; if you choose the right, I will go to the left." And so, Lot lifting up his eyes, saw all the region around the Jordan, which was entirely irrigated—before the Lord destroyed Sodom and Gomorrah—like the paradise of the Lord <note>A reference to the Garden of Eden.</note>, and like Egypt to those coming into Zoar <note>original: "segor"</note>. And Lot chose for himself the region around the Jordan and departed from the east. And they were separated from one another, brother from brother. Abram lived in the land of Canaan. But Lot stayed in the towns which were around the Jordan, and lived in Sodom. But the men of Sodom were very wicked and were great sinners before the Lord. And the Lord said to Abram, after Lot was separated from him: "Lift up your eyes directly..."

### Chapter 14

**A**nd it happened in that time that Amraphel, king of Shinar <note>original: "Sennaar," traditionally associated with Babylonia</note>; and Arioch, king of Pontus; and Chedorlaomer, king of the Elamites; and Tidal, king of the nations, went to war against Bera, king of Sodom; and against Birsha, king of Gomorrah; and against Shinab, king of Admah; and against Shemeber, king of Zeboiim; and against the king of Bela, which is Zoar <note>original: "segor"</note>. All these gathered in the woodland valley, which is now the Salt Sea <note>The Dead Sea</note>. For twelve years they had served Chedorlaomer, and in the thirteenth year they rebelled from him. Therefore, in the fourteenth year, Chedorlaomer came, and the kings who were with him: and they struck the Rephaim in Ashteroth Karnaim, and the Zuzim with them, and the Emim in Shaveh Kiriathaim, and the Horites in the mountains of Seir, as far as the plains of Paran, which is in the wilderness. And they returned, and came as far as the spring of Mishpat, which is Kadesh: and they struck all the region of the Amalekites, and the Amorites who lived in Hazezon-tamar. And the king of Sodom went out, and the king of Gomorrah, and the king of Admah, and the king of Zeboiim, and also the king of Bela which is Zoar: and they drew up their battle line against them in the woodland valley: specifically against Chedorlaomer, king of the Elamites; and Tidal, king of the nations; and Amraphel, king of Shinar; and Arioch, king of Pontus.

<column-break/>

Four kings against five. Now the woodland valley had many wells of bitumen <note>original: "bituminis," a naturally occurring tar or asphalt used in the ancient world for mortar and waterproofing</note>. Thus the king of Sodom and the king of Gomorrah turned their backs and fell there: and those who remained fled to the mountain. And they took all the goods of Sodom and Gomorrah, and everything pertaining to food, and went away. They also took Lot and his property, the son of Abram’s brother, who lived in Sodom.

And behold, one who had escaped reported this to Abram the Hebrew, who lived in the valley of Mamre the Amorite, the brother of Eshcol and the brother of Aner. For these had made a covenant with Abram. When Abram heard that his kinsman <margin>it was reported</margin> <note>original: "ephemizen," a Latinization of the Greek word meaning "to announce" or "to make known"</note> Lot was captured, he counted three hundred and eighteen of his trained servants born in his house, and pursued them as far as Dan. And having divided his companions, he fell upon them by night. And he struck them and pursued them as far as Hobah, which is to the left of Damascus. And he brought back all the goods: and Lot his brother <note>Though Lot was Abram's nephew, the term "fratrem" (brother) is often used for close male relatives</note> with his property, the women also and the people.

And the king of Sodom went out to meet him after he returned from the slaughter of Chedorlaomer and the kings who were with him, in the Valley of Shaveh <note>original: "sabe"</note>, which is the King's Valley. But indeed Melchizedek, king of Salem, bringing forth bread and wine—for he was the priest of the Most High God—blessed him and said: "Blessed be Abram by the Most High God, who created heaven and earth. And blessed be the Most High God, by whose protection your enemies are in your hands." And he gave him tithes <note>original: "decimas," a tenth part of the spoils of war</note> of everything. And the king of Sodom said to Abram: "Give me the souls <note>original: "animas," referring to the captured people</note>; take the rest for yourself." And he answered him: "I lift my hand to the Lord God the Most High, possessor of heaven and earth: that from a single thread even to a shoelace <note>original: "corrigiā calige"</note> I will not take from anything that is yours, lest you say: 'I have made Abram rich.' Except for those things which the young men have eaten, and the portions for the men who came with me."
