**After these things had transpired, Chapter 15.**

**A**fter these things, the word of the Lord came to Abram in a vision, saying: "Do not fear, Abram: I am your protector, and your reward shall be exceedingly great." And Abram said, "Lord God, what will you give me? I shall go without children, and the son of the steward of my house is this Eliezer of Damascus." And Abram added, "To me you have not given offspring, and behold, my household servant <note>original: "vernaculus," referring to a slave born within the master's house</note> shall be my heir." And immediately the word of the Lord came to him, saying, "This man shall not be your heir; but he who comes forth from your own body, him you shall have as your heir." And he led him outside and said to him, "Look up at the heavens and count the stars if you can." And he said to him, "So shall your offspring be." Abram believed God, and it was credited to him as righteousness. 

And he said to him, "I am the Lord who led you out of Ur of the Chaldeans <note>original: "hur chaldeor̄," an ancient city in Mesopotamia</note>, to give you this land so that you might possess it." But he said, "Lord God, how can I know that I will possess it?" And the Lord answered, "Take for me," he said, "a three-year-old heifer, a three-year-old goat, and a three-year-old ram, a turtledove also, and a pigeon." Taking all these, he divided them through the middle and placed both parts opposite one another. But the birds he did not divide. And birds of prey <note>original: "volucres," literally "flying things"</note> descended upon the carcasses, and Abram drove them away. 

And when the sun was setting, a deep sleep fell upon Abram, and a great and dark dread invaded him. And it was said to him, "Know for certain that your offspring shall be foreigners in a land not their own; and they shall subject them to slavery and afflict them for four hundred years. Nevertheless, I will judge the nation whom they shall serve; and after this they shall depart with great substance. But you shall go to your fathers in peace, buried in a good old age. In the fourth generation they shall return here. For the iniquities of the Amorites <note>A group of ancient people living in the Levant; the text implies their "cup of sin" is not yet full enough for God's judgment</note> are not yet complete up to the present time." 

When therefore the sun had set, a dark mist arose; and a smoking furnace appeared, and a lamp of fire passing between those divided parts. On that day the Lord made a covenant <note>original: "pepigit fed⁹," literally "struck a treaty"</note> with Abram, saying, "To your offspring I will give this land, from the river of Egypt to the great river Euphrates: the Kenites and the Kenizzites, the Kadmonites, and the Hittites, and the Perizzites, the Rephaim also, and the Amorites, and the Canaanites, and the Girgashites, and the Jebusites." 

**Chapter 16.**

**S**o Sarai, the wife of Abram, had not borne children; but having an Egyptian handmaid named Hagar, she said to her husband: "Behold, the Lord has closed me off from bearing children; go in to my handmaid, so that perhaps at least through her I may receive children." And when he agreed to her pleading, Sarai took Hagar the Egyptian, her handmaid—after ten years of beginning to live in the land of Canaan—and gave her to her husband as a wife. And he went in to her. 

But she, seeing that she had conceived, looked down upon her mistress. And Sarai said to Abram, "You act unjustly against me. I gave my handmaid into your bosom, and she, seeing that she has conceived, holds me in contempt. May the Lord judge between me and you." Abram responded to her, "Behold," he said, "your handmaid is in your hand; use her as you please." Therefore, when Sarai afflicted her, she took flight. 

And when the angel of the Lord found her beside a spring of water in the wilderness—which is on the way to Shur in the desert—he said to her, "Hagar, handmaid of Sarai, where do you come from, and where are you going?" She answered, "I am fleeing from the face of Sarai, my mistress." And the angel of the Lord said to her, "Return to your mistress, and humble yourself under her hand." And again he said, "Multiplying, I will multiply your offspring, and they shall not be counted for their multitude." And then he said, "Behold, you have conceived and shall bear a son; and you shall call his name Ishmael, because the Lord has heard your affliction. He shall be a wild man. His hand..."

**P**ost after he began to be ninety-nine years old, God appeared to him. **And** he said to him, "I am God Almighty <note>original: "deus omnipotens"</note>. **Walk** before me and be perfect. **And** I will place my covenant between me and you, and I will multiply you exceedingly." **Abram** fell face down on the ground. **And** God said to him, "It is I, and I will place my covenant with you, and you shall be the father of many nations. **No** longer shall your name be called Abram, but you shall be called Abraham, because I have established you as the father of many nations. **And** I will make you grow most greatly and I will place you among nations; and kings shall come forth from you. He replied, "Behold, she is in the tent." **The Lord** <note>the Latin text uses "cui" (to whom), but the context identifies the speaker as the Lord</note> said to him, "Returning, I will come to you at this time, life accompanying <note>original: "vita comite," an idiomatic expression meaning 'this time next year' or 'when the season returns'</note>, and Sarah your wife shall have a son." **Hearing** this, Sarah laughed behind the door of the tent. **Now** they were both old and of advanced age, and the way of women <note>a reference to the menstrual cycle and fertility</note> had ceased for Sarah. **She** laughed secretly, saying, "After I have grown old and my lord <note>referring to her husband, Abraham</note> is an old man, shall I give myself to pleasure?" **The** Lord said to Abraham, "Why did Sarah your wife laugh, saying, 'Shall I, an old woman, truly give birth?' **Is** anything difficult for God? What if thirty should be found there?" **He** replied, "I will not do it if I find thirty there." **"Since** I have once begun," he said, "I will speak to my Lord. **A**nd two angels came <insert>chapter 19</insert> to Sodom in the evening, while Lot was sitting in the gates of the city. When he had seen them, he rose and went to meet them. He worshipped prone to the ground and said, "**I** beseech you, lords, turn aside into the house of your servant <note>original: "pueri vestri," literally 'your boy' or 'your servant,' a common expression of humility</note> and stay there. Wash your feet, and in the morning you shall proceed on your way." They said, "No, but we will stay in the street." He urged them greatly to turn aside to him. When they had entered his house, he prepared a feast; he baked unleavened bread <note>original: "azima," bread made without yeast, typically prepared in haste or for religious purity</note> and they ate. But before they went to bed, the men of the city surrounded his house—from the young to the old, all the people together. **T**hey called to Lot ->.<- and said to him, "**W**here are the men who entered your house tonight? **B**ring them out here so that we may know them <note>original: "cognoscamus eos," a biblical euphemism for sexual relations</note>." Lot went out to them, closing the door behind his back, and said, "**D**o not, I pray you, my brothers; do not do this evil. **I** have two daughters who have not yet known a man. **I** will bring them out to you, and you may use them as it pleases you, provided you do nothing of harm to these men, because they have entered under the shadow of my roof." **B**ut they said, "**S**tand back!" And again: "**Y**ou entered here," they said, "as a stranger; is it now as a judge? **W**e will therefore afflict you yourself even more than these men." **A**nd they pressed against Lot most violently. **A**nd they were already near to breaking down the doors, when behold, the men [the angels] reached out their hands and pulled Lot inside to them, and they shut the door. **A**nd those who were outside they struck with blindness, from the smallest to the greatest, so that they could not find the door. **T**hey said then to Lot, "Do you have anyone here of your 

<column-break/>

own—sons-in-law, or sons, or daughters? **A**ll who are yours, lead them out of this city. **F**or we will destroy this place because the cry of them has grown loud before the Lord, who has sent us to destroy them." **S**o Lot went out and spoke to his sons-in-law, who were to take his daughters in marriage, and said, "**A**rise and get out of this place, for the Lord will destroy this city." **A**nd he seemed to them to be speaking as if he were joking. **A**nd when it was morning, the angels urged him, saying, "**A**rise, take your wife and the two daughters you have, lest you also perish in the wickedness of the city." **W**hile he lingered, they took his hand and the hand of his wife and of his two daughters, because the Lord was sparing him. **T**hey led him out and placed him outside the city; there they spoke to him, saying, "**S**ave your life. **D**o not look behind your back, nor stay in all the surrounding region, but save yourself in the mountain, lest you also perish at the same time." **A**nd Lot said to them, "**I** pray you, my lord, because your servant has found grace before you, and you have magnified your glory and your mercy which you have shown me by saving my life; but I cannot be saved in the mountain, lest perhaps evil overtake me and I die. **T**here is a city nearby to which I can flee, a small one, and I shall be safe in it. **I**s it not a small thing? And my soul shall live." **A**nd he said to him, "**B**ehold, even in this I have heard your prayers, that I will not overturn the city for which you have spoken. **H**urry and be saved there, because I cannot do anything until you enter it." **F**or that reason, the name of that city was called Segor <note>original: "Segor," the Latin form of the Hebrew 'Zoar,' meaning 'small' or 'insignificant'</note>. **T**he sun had risen over the earth when Lot entered Segor. **T**herefore the Lord rained upon Sodom and Gomorrah sulfur and fire from the Lord out of heaven. **A**nd he overturned these cities, and all the surrounding region, all the inhabitants of the cities, and all things that grow upon the earth. **A**nd looking back...

# . Genesis .

His wife, looking back behind him, was turned into a statue of salt. **A**braham, rising early in the morning to the place where he had stood before with the Lord, gazed toward Sodom and Gomorrah and the entire land of that region. And he saw the ashes ascending from the earth like the smoke of a furnace. For when the Lord overthrew the cities of that region, he remembered Abraham and delivered Lot from the destruction of the cities in which he had lived. 

And Lot went up from Zoar <note>original: "segor," the small city the angels allowed Lot to flee to as a refuge</note> and stayed in the mountain, and his two daughters were with him. For he was afraid to stay in Zoar; and he stayed in a cave, he and his two daughters with him. And the elder said to the younger: "Our father is old, and no man remains in the land who can come in to us according to the custom of the whole earth. Come, let us make him drunk with wine and sleep with him, so that we may preserve offspring from our father." 

And so they gave their father wine to drink that night. And the elder entered and slept with her father; but he did not perceive either when the daughter lay down or when she arose. And on the next day, the elder said to the younger: "Behold, I slept yesterday with my father; let us give him wine to drink this night as well, and you shall sleep with him so that we may save offspring from our father." 

And they gave their father wine to drink that night also. And the younger daughter entered and slept with him. And even then he did not perceive when she lay down or when she arose. Therefore the two daughters of Lot conceived by their father. And the elder bore a son and called his name Moab <note>This name was traditionally interpreted to mean "from the father"</note>. He is the father of the Moabites to the present day. And the younger bore a son and called his name Ammon—that is, "the son of my people"—and he is the father of the Ammonites until today. 

**ca. xx.** <note>Chapter 20</note>
**P**rofectus <note>original: "Profectus inde abraham," Abraham set out from there</note> set out from there into the southern land and lived between Kadesh and Shur, and he stayed as a traveler in Gerar. And he said of Sarah his wife: "She is my sister." 

Therefore Abimelech, king of Gerar, sent and took her. But God came to Abimelech in a dream by night and said to him: "Behold, you shall die because of the woman you have taken, for she has a husband." But Abimelech had not touched her, and he said: "Lord, will you kill even an ignorant and just nation? Did he not say to me, 'She is my sister'? And she herself said, 'He is my brother'? In the simplicity of my heart and the purity of my hands I have done this." 

And God said to him: "I also know that you did this with a simple heart; and therefore I kept you from sinning against me, and I did not allow you to touch her. Now therefore, return the wife to the man, for he is a prophet <note>The first time the word "propheta" is used in the Bible to describe a person</note>, and he shall pray for you and you shall live. But if you will not return her, know that you shall surely die, you and all that are yours." 

And immediately rising in the night, Abimelech called all his servants and spoke all these words in their ears. And all the men were greatly afraid. But Abimelech also called Abraham and said to him: "What have you done to us? How have we sinned against you, that you have brought upon me and my kingdom a grave sin? You have done to us things that you ought not to have done." 

And questioning him again, he said: "What did you see that you should do this?" Abraham responded: "I thought to myself, saying: 'Perhaps there is no fear of God in this place, and they will kill me because of my wife.' But otherwise, she truly is my sister: the daughter of my father, but not the daughter of my mother, and I took her as a wife. And after God led me out of my father's house, I said to her: 'This mercy you shall do for me: in every place where we enter, you shall say that I am your brother.'" 

Abimelech therefore took sheep and oxen and servants and handmaids and gave them to Abraham. And he returned Sarah his wife to him and said: "The land is before you; live wherever it pleases you." But to Sarah he said: "Behold, I have given a thousand pieces of silver to your brother. This shall be to you..."

### Chapter 22.

<margin>ar</margin>

**A**fter these things were done, God tested Abraham and said to him: "Abraham, Abraham." He replied: "Here I am." He said to him: "Take your son, your only son Isaac whom you love, and go into the land of vision <note>original: 'terram visionis', often identified as Mount Moriah</note>, and there you shall offer him as a burnt offering <term>holocaustum: a religious sacrifice where the animal is entirely consumed by fire</term> upon one of the mountains that I will show you." Therefore, Abraham, rising in the night, saddled his donkey, taking with him two young men and Isaac his son. And when he had split the wood for the burnt offering, he went to the place God had commanded him. On the third day, raising his eyes, he saw the place from afar. And he said to his young men: "Wait here with the donkey; I and the boy will hasten over there, and after we have worshipped, we will return to you." And he took the wood for the burnt offering and placed it upon his son Isaac. He himself carried the fire and the sword in his hands. And as the two walked together, Isaac said to his father: "My father?" And he replied: "What do you wish, my son?" "Behold," he said, "the fire and the wood; where is the victim for the burnt offering?" Abraham said: "God will provide for himself the victim for the burnt offering, my son." So they went on together and came to the place God had shown him, where he built an altar and arranged the wood upon it. And when he had bound his son Isaac, he laid him on the altar upon the pile of wood. And he stretched out his hand and took the sword to sacrifice his son. And behold, an angel of the Lord called from heaven, saying: "Abraham, Abraham!" And he replied: "Here I am." And he said to him: "Do not stretch out your hand against the boy, nor do anything to him. Now I know that you fear God, and you have not spared your only son for my sake." Abraham lifted up his eyes and saw behind his back a ram caught by its horns among the briars; taking it, he offered it as a burnt offering instead of his son. And he called the name of that place "The Lord Sees" <note>original: 'Dominus videt', equivalent to the Hebrew 'Jehovah-jireh'</note>. Whence it is said to this day: "On the mountain, the Lord will see." Then the angel of the Lord called Abraham a second time from heaven, saying: "By myself I have sworn, says the Lord, because you have done this thing and have not spared your only son for my sake: I will bless you and multiply your seed like the stars of heaven and like the sand on the seashore. Your seed shall possess the gates of their enemies, and in your seed all nations of the earth shall be blessed, because you have obeyed my voice." Abraham returned to his young men, and they went together to Beersheba, and he lived there. After these things, it was told to Abraham that Milcah had also borne sons to his brother Nahor: Uz the firstborn, and Buz his brother, and Kemuel the father of the Syrians, and Chesed, and Hazo, and Pildash, and Jidlaph, and Bethuel—from whom Rebekah was born. These eight Milcah bore to Nahor, Abraham's brother. His concubine, named Reumah, also bore Tebah, Gaham, Tahash, and Maacah.

And Ephron answered Abraham, while all those who entered the gate of his city were listening, saying: "Let it not be so, my lord, but rather listen to what I say. I give the field to you, and the cave that is in it; in the presence of the sons of my people, bury your dead." I will give money for the field; receive it, and so I will bury my dead in the field." And Ephron answered: "My lord, hear me. The land you ask for is worth four hundred shekels <term>siclos: an ancient unit of weight used for silver currency</term> of silver; that is the price between me and you. But how much is that? Bury your dead." When Abraham had heard this, he weighed out the money that Ephron had requested in the hearing of the sons of Heth: four hundred shekels of silver, in certified public currency <term>probate monete publice: coinage that is officially recognized and standardized for trade</term>. And the field which once belonged to Ephron, in which there was a double cave <note>original: 'spelunca duplex', traditionally known as the Cave of Machpelah</note> looking toward Mamre—both the field and the cave, and all the trees in it throughout all its borders round about—was confirmed as Abraham's possession, in the sight of the sons of Heth and all who entered the gate of that city. And so Abraham buried his wife Sarah in the double cave of the field that looked toward Mamre. This is Hebron, in the land of Canaan. And the field and the pit that was in it were confirmed to Abraham by the sons of Heth as his possession for a monument <note>original: 'monumenti', here meaning a permanent tomb or memorial</note>.

### Chapter 24.

**A**braham was now old and of many days, and the Lord had blessed him in all things. And he said to the eldest servant of his house, who was in charge of all he had: "Put your hand under my thigh <note>an ancient Near Eastern gesture for swearing a most solemn and binding oath</note>, so that I may make you swear by the Lord, the God of heaven and earth, that you will not take a wife for my son from the daughters of the Canaanites

<column-break/>

among whom I dwell. Instead, you shall go to my own land and kindred, and from there you shall take a wife for my son Isaac." The servant replied: "If the woman is unwilling to come with me into this land, must I lead your son back to the place from which you came out?" And Abraham said: "Beware that you never lead my son back there. The Lord God of heaven and earth, who took me from my father’s house and from the land of my birth, who spoke to me and swore to me, saying, 'To your offspring I will give this land'—He himself will send His angel before you, and you shall take a wife for my son from there. But if the woman is unwilling to follow you, you shall not be bound by this oath; only do not lead my son back there." Therefore, the servant put his hand under the thigh of Abraham his lord and swore to him concerning this matter. And he took ten camels from his lord’s herd and departed, carrying with him all his lord’s goods. He set out and traveled to Mesopotamia, to the city of Nahor. And when he had made the camels kneel down outside the town, near a well of water, in the evening—at the time when women are accustomed to come out to draw water—he prayed to the Lord, saying: "O Lord God of my lord Abraham, meet me today, I beseech you, and show mercy to my lord Abraham. Behold, I stand by the spring of water, and the daughters of the inhabitants of this city will come out to draw water. Therefore, the girl to whom I shall say, 'Tip your pitcher so that I may drink,' and she answers, 'Drink, and I will also give your camels a drink'—let her be the one you have prepared for your servant Isaac. By this I shall understand that you have shown mercy to my lord Abraham." He had not yet finished these words within himself, and behold, Rebecca came out, the daughter of Bethuel, the son of Milcah, who was the wife of Nahor, Abraham’s brother. She carried a pitcher on her shoulder. She was a girl of exceeding beauty, a most fair virgin, and unknown to man.

Laban and Bethuel answered: "This word has come from the Lord. We cannot speak anything else to you beyond his pleasure. Look, Rebecca is before you; take her and go, and let her be the wife of your master’s son, just as the Lord has spoken." When Abraham's servant heard this, he bowed down to the earth and worshipped the Lord. Bringing out vessels of silver and gold, and garments, he gave them to Rebecca as a gift. He also offered gifts to her brothers and her mother. A feast was begun, and eating and drinking together, they stayed there. But rising in the morning, the servant spoke: "Let me go, that I may return to my master." Her brothers and mother answered: "Let the girl stay with us at least ten days, and after that she shall depart." He said: "Do not hold me back, for the Lord has directed my way. Let me go, so that I may proceed to my master." And they said: "Let us call the girl and ask her own will." And when she had been called and arrived, they asked: "Will you go with this man?" She said: "I will go." So they sent her away, along with her nurse, Abraham's servant, and his companions, praying for prosperity for their sister and saying: "You are our sister. May you grow into thousands of thousands, and may your offspring possess the gates of their enemies." Then Rebecca and her maids, having mounted the camels, followed the man, who was returning in haste to his master. At that time, Isaac was walking

<column-break/>

along the way that leads to the well, the name of which is "of the living and seeing" <note>original: 'viventis et videntis', the Latin translation of the Hebrew 'Beer-lahai-roi'</note>. For he lived in the southern land. He had gone out to meditate in the field as the day was already declining. And when he lifted his eyes, he saw camels coming from afar. Rebecca also, when she saw Isaac, descended from the camel and said to the servant: "Who is that man who comes through the field to meet us?" And he said to her: "He is my master." But she, quickly taking her cloak, covered herself. Then the servant told his master Isaac everything he had done. Isaac led her into the tent of Sarah his mother and took her as his wife; and he loved her so much that it tempered the grief that had come from his mother's death. **Chapter 25.**

<insert>A</insert>braham took another wife named Keturah, who bore him Zimran, Jokshan, Medan, Midian, Ishbak, and Shuah. Jokshan also fathered Sheba and Dedan. The sons of Dedan were Asshurim, Letushim, and Leummim. But from Midian were born Ephah, Epher, Hanoch, Abida, and Eldaah. All these were the sons of Keturah. Abraham gave all that he possessed to Isaac; but to the sons of his concubines <note>referring to Hagar and Keturah</note> he bestowed gifts, and while he was still living, he separated them from his son Isaac toward the eastern region. The days of Abraham’s life were one hundred and seventy-five years; and failing, he died in a good old age, advanced in years and full of days, and was gathered to his people. His sons Isaac and Ishmael buried him in the double cave <term>spelunca duplici: also known as the Cave of Machpelah</term>, which is situated in the field of Ephron, son of Zohar the Hittite, opposite Mamre, which he had bought from the sons of Heth. There he was buried, along with his wife Sarah. And after his death, God blessed Isaac his son, who lived near the well named "of the living and seeing." These are the generations of Ishmael

## ->Genesis.<-

sons of Abraham whom Hagar the Egyptian, Sarah's servant, bore to him. And these are the names of his sons by their names and their generations. The firstborn of Ishmael was Nabaioth; then Kedar, Adbeel, Mibsam, Mishma, also Dumah and Massa, Hadad, Tema, Jetur, Naphish, and Kedemah. These are the sons of Ishmael, and these are their names according to their villages and towns: twelve princes of their tribes. And the years of Ishmael's life reached one hundred and thirty-seven; failing, he died, and was gathered to his people <note>original: 'appositus ad populum' — a biblical idiom for dying and joining one's ancestors in the afterlife</note>. He lived from Havilah all the way to Shur, which faces Egypt as one enters toward the Assyrians. He died in the presence of all his brothers. <margin>chapter 25</margin> These also are the generations of Isaac, the son of Abraham. Abraham fathered Isaac. When he was forty years old, he took as his wife Rebecca, the daughter of Bethuel the Syrian from Mesopotamia, the sister of Laban. And Isaac prayed to the Lord for his wife, because she was barren. The Lord heard him and granted conception to Rebecca; but the little ones struggled together in her womb. She said, "If it was to be thus with me, why was it necessary to conceive?" And she went to consult the Lord. He, answering, said: "Two nations are in your womb, and two peoples shall be divided from your belly; one people shall overcome the other, and the elder shall serve the younger." Now the time for her to give birth had arrived, and behold, twins were found in her womb. The one who came out first was red, and hairy all over like a skin cloak <note>original: 'in morem pellis hispidus' — suggesting a rough, fur-like appearance</note>; and his name was called Esau. Immediately, the other came out holding his brother's heel in his hand; and for that reason, he called him Jacob <note>The name Jacob (Ya'aqov) is a Hebrew pun on the word for 'heel' (aqev)</note>. Isaac was sixty years old when the little ones were born. When they grew up, Esau became a man skilled in hunting and a man of the field; but Jacob was a simple man dwelling in tents. Isaac loved Esau because he ate of his hunting, and

<column-break/>

Rebecca loved Jacob. Now Jacob cooked a stew. When Esau had come in from the field exhausted, he said: "Give me some of this red cooking, for I am exceedingly tired." For this reason, his name was called Edom <note>original: 'Edom' — meaning 'Red', another pun on the red stew and Esau's appearance</note>. Jacob said to him: "Sell me your birthright <note>original: 'primogenita' — the legal rights and inheritance belonging to the firstborn son</note>." He answered: "Look, I am dying. What will the birthright profit me?" Jacob said: "Swear then to me." Esau swore to him and sold his birthright; and so, having received bread and a meal of lentils, he ate and drank and went his way, making little account of having sold his birthright. **chapter 26**
**N**ow a famine arose in the land, after that barrenness which had occurred in the days of Abraham. Isaac went to Abimelech, king of the Philistines, in Gerar. And the Lord appeared to him and said: "Do not go down into Egypt, but rest in the land which I shall tell you, and live there as a stranger. I will be with you and bless you. For to you and your seed I will give all these regions, fulfilling the oath which I promised to Abraham your father. And I will multiply your seed like the stars of heaven. I will give to your descendants all these regions, and in your seed all the nations of the earth shall be blessed, because Abraham obeyed my voice and kept my precepts and my commands, and observed my ceremonies and laws." So Isaac remained in Gerar. When he was questioned by the men of that place about his wife, he answered: "She is my sister." For he had feared to confess that she was joined to him in marriage, thinking that they might perhaps kill him because of her beauty. And when many days had passed and he stayed there, Abimelech, king of the Philistines, looking through a window, saw him playing <note>original: 'iocantem' — here implying amorous or intimate play between a husband and wife</note> with Rebecca his wife. Having called for him, he said: "It is clear that she is your wife. Why did you lie and say she was your sister?" He answered: "I feared lest I might die on her account."

Because of this, the Philistines envied him, and they blocked all the wells which his father Abraham's servants had dug at that time, filling them with earth, so much so that Abimelech himself said to Isaac, "Depart from us, for you have become much more powerful than we are." And departing, so that he might come to the valley <note>original: 'torrente' — referring to a wadi or seasonal stream bed</note> of Gerar and live there, he dug other wells again, which his father Abraham's servants had dug, and which the Philistines had long ago blocked after his death. And he called them by the same names that his father had called them before. And they dug in the valley and found living water <note>original: 'aquam vivam' — meaning a natural flowing spring as opposed to collected rainwater</note>. But there also was a quarrel of the shepherds of Gerar against the shepherds of Isaac, saying, "The water is ours." For which reason, he called the name of the well, from what had happened, "Calumny" <note>original: 'calumniam' — meaning a false accusation or dispute; identifying the well known as Esek</note>. And they replied: "We have seen that the Lord is with you, and therefore we said: Let there be an oath between us, and let us enter into a covenant, that you will do no evil to us, just as we have touched nothing of yours, nor done anything that would harm you, but sent you away in peace, increased by the blessing of the Lord." So he made a feast for them. And after food and drink, rising in the morning, they swore to one another; and Isaac sent them away peacefully to their own place. And behold, on that same day, Isaac's servants came, announcing to him about the well they had dug, and saying: "We have found water." Wherefore he called it "Abundance" <note>original: 'abundantiam' — relating to the name Shebah</note>, and the name of the city was given as Beersheba <note>meaning 'Well of the Oath'</note> even to the present day. ### Chapter 27.

### Chapter 28

Therefore Isaac called Jacob and blessed him; and he commanded him, saying: "Do not take a wife from the race of Canaan, but go and travel into Mesopotamia of Syria <note>original: 'mesopotamiam syrie'</note> to the house of Bethuel, your mother's father, and take for yourself a wife from there, from the daughters of Laban your uncle. And may God Almighty bless you, and make you grow and multiply, so that you may become a multitude of peoples. And may He give you the blessings of Abraham and to your offspring after you, so that you may possess the land of your pilgrimage, which He promised to your grandfather." And when Isaac had sent him away, he departed and came to Mesopotamia of Syria, to Laban the son of Bethuel the Syrian, the brother of Rebekah his mother. Now Esau, seeing that his father had blessed Jacob and sent him into Mesopotamia of Syria to take a wife from there, and that after the blessing he had commanded him saying, "you shall not take a wife from the daughters of Canaan," and that Jacob, obeying his parents, had gone into Syria—and realizing also that his father did not look favorably upon the daughters of Canaan—he went to Ishmael and took as a wife (in addition to those he already had) Mahalath <note>original: 'meleth'</note>, the daughter of Ishmael, son of Abraham, the sister of Nebajoth.

Therefore Jacob, having departed from Beersheba, went toward Haran. And when he had come to a certain place and wished to rest in it after sunset, he took one of the stones that lay there and, placing it under his head, he slept in that same place. And he saw in his dreams a ladder standing upon the earth, and its top touching heaven; and also the angels of God ascending and descending by it. And the Lord, leaning upon the ladder, was saying to him: "I am the Lord God of Abraham your father, and the God of Isaac. The land on which you sleep, I will give to you and to your offspring. And your offspring shall be like the dust of the earth. You shall spread to the east and the west, and the north and the south; and in you and in your offspring all the tribes of the earth shall be blessed. And I will be your guardian wherever you go, and I will bring you back into this land; nor will I leave you until I have fulfilled everything I have said." And when Jacob had awakened from sleep, he said: "Truly the Lord is in this place, and I did not know it." And being afraid, he said: "How terrible <note>original: 'terribilis' — in a medieval context, this means 'awe-inspiring' or 'full of majesty' rather than 'bad'</note> is this place! This is nothing else but the house of God and the gate of heaven." Rising therefore in the morning, Jacob took the stone he had placed under his head and set it up as a monument <note>original: 'titulum' — a sacred pillar or marker</note>, pouring oil upon it. And he called the name of the city Bethel <note>Bethel means 'House of God'</note>, which before was called Luz. He also made a vow, saying: "If the Lord will be with me and guard me in the way in which I walk, and give..."

**P**roceeding therefore, Jacob came into the land of the East. And he saw a well in a field, and three flocks of sheep lying near it. For from that well the livestock were watered, and its mouth was closed by a great stone. It was the custom that when all the sheep were gathered, they would roll away the stone; and once the flocks were refreshed, they would place it back over the mouth of the well. And he said to the shepherds: "Brothers, where are you from?" They replied: "From Haran." **Interrogating** them, he said: "Do you know Laban, the son of Nahor?" And Jacob said: "**Much** of the day still remains, nor is it yet time to lead the flocks back to the folds. Give the sheep water first, and then lead them back to pasture." They responded: "**We cannot**, until all the livestock are gathered and we move the stone from the mouth of the well, so that we may water the flocks." They were still speaking when behold, Rachel came with the sheep of her father, for she herself tended the flock. **When** Jacob had seen her, and knew her to be his cousin <note>original: 'consobrinam' — specifically his mother's brother's daughter</note>, and the sheep to be those of Laban his uncle: he moved the stone by which the well was closed. And once the flock was watered, he kissed her. **And** raising his voice, he wept; and he indicated to her that he was the brother of her father <note>In biblical genealogy, 'brother' often refers broadly to a close male relative or kinsman; Jacob is actually Laban’s nephew.</note> and the son of Rebekah. Jacob, loving her, said: "**I will serve** you for Rachel, your younger daughter, for seven years." **Laban** replied: "**It is better** that I give her to you than to another man. **Stay** with me." **Jacob served** therefore for Rachel for seven years; and they seemed to him but a few days because of the greatness of his love. **And he said** to Laban: "**Give** me my wife, for now the time is fulfilled, so that I may go in to her." **He**, having called a great crowd of friends to a feast, held the wedding. **And** in the evening, he brought in Leah his daughter to him, giving a handmaid named Zilpah to his daughter. **When** Jacob had gone in to her according to custom, when morning came, he saw it was Leah. And he said to his father-in-law: "**What** is this that you intended to do? **Did I not** serve you for Rachel? **Why** have you deceived me?" **Laban** replied: "**It is not** the custom in our place to give the younger in marriage before the firstborn. **Complete** the week of days for this union <note>The wedding feast typically lasted seven days.</note>, and I will give this one to you also, for the work with which you shall serve me for another seven years." **He agreed** to the deal; and when the week had passed, he took Rachel as his wife. **To her**, her father had given the servant Bilhah. **And at last**, having obtained the marriage he desired, he preferred the love of the second over the first, serving with him for another seven years. **The Lord**, seeing that he despised Leah, opened her womb, while her sister remained barren. **She**, having conceived, gave birth to a son, and called his name Reuben, saying: "**The Lord has seen** my humiliation. **Now** my husband will love me." **Again** she conceived, and bore a son, and said: "**Because** the Lord heard that I was held in contempt, he has given this one also to me." **And she called** his name [Simeon...]

[...named] him Simeon. And she conceived a third time and bore another son, and said: "Now my husband will be joined to me, because I have borne him three sons." And for this reason, she called his name Levi <note>The name Levi is related to the Hebrew word for "joined" or "attached," reflecting Leah's hope for a stronger bond with Jacob.</note>. A fourth time she conceived and bore a son and said: "Now I will praise the Lord." And for this reason, she called him Judah <note>Judah, or *Yehudah*, is derived from the word for "praise."</note>. And she ceased from bearing.

<insert>Chapter 30</insert>

**B**ut Rachel, seeing that she was barren, envied her sister and said to her husband: "Give me children, otherwise I shall die." To whom Jacob, being angry, responded: "Am I as God, who has deprived you of the fruit of your womb?" But she said: "I have a handmaid, Bilhah; go in to her that she may bear upon my knees <note>To bear a child "upon the knees" was a legal custom of adoption, where the child of a handmaid was formally recognized as the child of the mistress.</note>, and I may have children by her." And she gave him Bilhah as a wife. And after she had gone in to the man, she conceived and bore a son. And Rachel said: "The Lord has judged for me, and has heard my voice, giving me a son." And for this reason, she called his name Dan. Again Bilhah, conceiving, bore another, for whom Rachel said: "The Lord has compared me with my sister <note>original: 'comparavit me' — often interpreted as 'wrestled' or 'contended'</note> and I have prevailed." And she called him Naphtali. Leah, seeing that she had ceased from bearing, gave Zilpah her handmaid to her husband. When she gave birth to a son after conception, Leah said: "Happily." And for this reason, she called his name Gad. And Zilpah bore another; and Leah said: "This is for my blessedness. For all women shall call me blessed." Therefore she called him Asher. Now Reuben, going out in the time of the wheat harvest into the field, found mandrakes <note>original: 'mandragoras' — plants with root systems resembling human forms, historically believed to aid fertility and act as an aphrodisiac</note>, which he brought to his mother Leah. And Rachel said: "Give me part of your son's mandrakes." She replied: "Does it seem a small thing to you that you have taken my husband from me, unless you also take my son's mandrakes?" Rachel said: "Let him sleep with you this night for your son's mandrakes." And as Jacob was returning from the field in the evening, Leah went out to meet him and said:

---

"You shall come in to me," she said, "because I have hired you for my son's mandrakes." And he slept with her that night; and God heard her prayers. And she conceived and bore a fifth son, and said: "God has given me my reward, because I gave my handmaid to my husband." And she called his name Issachar. Again Leah, conceiving, bore a sixth son and said: "God has endowed me with a good dowry. This time also my husband shall be with me, because I have borne him six sons." And for this reason, she called his name Zebulun. After him she bore a daughter named Dinah. And the Lord, remembering Rachel, heard her and opened her womb; she conceived and bore a son, saying: "God has taken away my reproach." And she called his name Joseph, saying: "May the Lord add another to me." Now when Joseph was born, Jacob said to his father-in-law: "Send me away, that I may return to my father's house and to my land. Give me my wives and my children for whom I have served you, so that I may depart. For you know the service with which I have served you."

<margin>35 Folios</margin>
Laban said to him: "Let me find grace in your sight. I have learned by experience that God has blessed me because of you. Name your wages that I should give you." But he replied: "You know how I have served you, and how great your possession has been in my hands. You had little before I came to you, and now you have become rich; and the Lord has blessed you at my coming. It is just, therefore, that I should at some time provide for my own house." And Laban said: "What shall I give you?" But he said: "I want nothing; but if you will do what I ask, I will again feed and keep your flocks. Go through all your flocks and separate all the sheep that are speckled and of various colors, and whatever is dark and spotted and speckled, both among the sheep and among the goats; this shall be my wages. And my justice shall answer for me tomorrow, when the appointed time [comes]..."

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Jacob’s service to his uncle Laban. Following the birth of his children, Jacob has negotiated a specific wage—the spotted and dark-colored animals from Laban's flocks—and is now using various methods to ensure his portion of the flock grows strong and numerous.</meta>

# ->Genesis<-

...comes before you: and all that are not variegated and spotted and dark—among the sheep as well as the goats—shall prove me a thief <note>Jacob is proposing a deal where his wages consist only of the "imperfect" or multi-colored animals, making it easy to see if he has stolen any of Laban's pure-colored flock.</note>. And Laban said: "I am pleased with what you ask." And he separated on that day the goats, and the sheep, and the he-goats, and the rams, that were variegated and spotted. But the entire flock of one color—that is, of white and black fleece—he delivered into the hands of his sons; and he put a distance of three days' journey between himself and his son-in-law, who fed the rest of his flocks.

Therefore, Jacob took green rods of poplar, and almond, and plane trees, and partially peeled them. When the bark was pulled off, whiteness appeared in the parts that were stripped; but those parts that remained whole stayed green. And in this way, a variegated color was produced. And he placed the rods in the troughs where the water was poured: so that when the flocks came to drink, they would have the rods before their eyes, and would conceive in the sight of them. And it happened that in the heat of mating, the sheep looked at the rods and brought forth spotted, variegated, and diverse-colored offspring. 

And Jacob divided the flock, and placed the rods in the troughs before the eyes of the rams. Now, those that were white and black belonged to Laban, but the rest to Jacob, the flocks being separated from each other. Therefore, when it was the first season for the sheep to mate, Jacob placed the rods in the water troughs before the eyes of the rams and sheep, so that they might conceive while looking at them; but when the late mating season came, and the later conceptions, he did not place them. And so it happened that the late-born ones belonged to Laban, and those of the first season to Jacob. And the man was enriched beyond measure; and he had many flocks, handmaids and servants, camels and donkeys.

<margin>36</margin>
**Chapter 31**

**A**fter he heard the words of Laban’s sons saying: "Jacob has taken everything that was our father's, and from his wealth he has become famous and enriched"; and he noticed the face of Laban, that it was not toward him as it was yesterday and the day before <note>original: "heri et nudiustercius"—a Latin idiom meaning "recently" or "in times past."</note>; especially since the Lord said to him: "Return to the land of your fathers and to your kindred, and I will be with you." 

He sent and called Rachel and Leah into the field where he fed the flocks, and said to them: "I see your father’s face, that it is not toward me as it was yesterday and the day before. But the God of my father has been with me; and you yourselves know that I have served your father with all my strength. But your father has cheated me and changed my wages ten times: and yet God did not permit him to hurt me. Whenever he said: 'The variegated ones shall be your wages,' all the sheep bore variegated young; but when he said to the contrary: 'You shall take all the white ones for your wages,' all the flocks brought forth white ones. And so God has taken the substance <note>In this context, "substance" refers to wealth and livestock.</note> of your father and given it to me. 

After the time for the sheep to conceive had come, I lifted up my eyes and saw in a dream the males leaping upon the females, variegated and spotted and of diverse colors. And the angel of the Lord said to me in a dream: 'Jacob.' And I answered: 'I am here.' And he said: 'Lift up your eyes and see all the males leaping upon the females, variegated, spotted, and speckled. For I have seen all that Laban has done to you. I am the God of Bethel, where you anointed the stone and made a vow to me. Now therefore, arise and go out of this land, returning to the land of your birth.'"

And Rachel and Leah answered: "Have we anything left in the wealth and inheritance of our father’s house? Has he not treated us as strangers, and sold us, and consumed our price? But God has taken the riches of our father and delivered them to us and to our children. Therefore, do everything that the Lord has commanded you." Then Jacob arose, and having placed his children and his wives upon camels, he departed, taking all his substance and flocks.

And as he searched the whole tent and found nothing, she said: "**Let** my lord not be angry that I cannot rise before you, for the custom of women <note>A polite reference to menstruation</note> has now happened to me." **Thus** the seeker’s diligence was frustrated. **And** Jacob, swelling with anger, said in a quarrel: "**For** what fault of mine and for what sin of mine have you so burned after me, and searched all my household goods? But when Hamor, the father of Shechem <note>original: "sichem"</note>, had gone out to speak to Jacob, behold, his sons were coming from the field; and having heard what had happened, they were very angry because he had performed a foul deed in Israel, and by violating Jacob's daughter, had committed an illicit act. Therefore Hamor spoke to them: "The soul of my son Shechem has clung to your daughter. Give her to him as a wife, and let us join in marriages with one another. But Shechem also said to her father and her brothers: "Let me find grace before you, and whatever you decide, I will give. Increase the dowry and demand gifts, and I will freely grant whatever you ask; only give me this girl as a wife." The sons of Jacob answered Shechem and his father deceitfully, being enraged by the ravishing of their sister: "We cannot do what you ask, nor give our sister to an uncircumcised man, which is illicit and wicked among us. But in this we can be allied: if you will be like us, and every male among you is circumcised, then we will give and take our daughters and yours mutually; and we will dwell with you, and we will be one people. But if you will not be circumcised, we will take our daughter and depart." Their offering pleased Hamor and Shechem his son; nor did the young man delay in fulfilling what was asked immediately. Let us only acquiesce in this, and dwelling together, let us become one people." And all were in agreement, all the males being circumcised. When they had departed, the other sons of Jacob rushed upon the slain and plundered the city in revenge for the rape: their sheep and herds and donkeys, and wasting everything that was in the houses and in the fields; and they led away their little ones and wives as captives. When these things were boldly perpetrated, Jacob said to Simeon and Levi: "You have troubled me, and made me hateful to the Canaanites <note>original: "chananeis"</note> and Perizzites <note>original: "pherezeis"</note>, the inhabitants of this land. We are few; they will gather together and strike me, and I and my house shall be destroyed." They replied: "Should they have abused our sister like a harlot?"

At that same time, Deborah, Rebecca's nurse, died and was buried at the foot of Bethel under an oak; and the name of that place was called "The Oak of Weeping" <note>original: "quercus fletus"</note>. Now God appeared to Jacob again after he returned from Mesopotamia of Syria <note>original: "mesopotamia syrie," referring to Paddan-Aram</note>, and he came to Bethel; and He blessed him, saying: "You shall no longer be called Jacob, but Israel shall be your name." And He called him Israel. And He said to him: "I am God Almighty <note>original: "deus omnipotens"</note>. Increase and multiply: nations and peoples of nations shall come from you. Kings shall come forth from your loins. And the land which I gave to Abraham and Isaac, I will give to you and to your offspring after you." And He departed from him. But Jacob erected a stone pillar in the place where God had spoken to him, offering drink offerings upon it and pouring oil; and he called the name of that place Bethel. And departing from there, he came in the springtime to the land that leads to Ephrath. While Rachel was giving birth there, she began to be in danger due to the difficulty of the labor. And the midwife said to her: "Do not fear, for you shall have this son as well." But as her soul was departing because of the pain, and death was now imminent, she called the name of her son Ben-Oni, which is "The Son of my Sorrow" <note>original: "bennoni : id est filius doloris mei"</note>. His father, however, called him Benjamin, which is "The Son of the Right Hand" <note>original: "bejamin : id est filius dextere"</note>. So Rachel died and was buried on the road that leads to Ephrath; this is Bethlehem. And Jacob erected a pillar over her grave. This is the pillar of Rachel's monument even to the present day. Departing from there, he pitched his tent beyond the "Tower of the Flock" <note>original: "turrem gregis," or Migdal Eder</note>. <column-break/> And while he was living in that region, Reuben went and slept with Bilhah <note>original: "bala"</note>, his father’s concubine; and this did not escape his father's notice. Now the sons of Jacob were twelve. The sons of Leah: the firstborn Reuben, and Simeon, and Levi, Judah, and Issachar, and Zebulun. The sons of Rachel: Joseph and Benjamin. The sons of Bilhah, Rachel's handmaid: Dan and Naphtali. The sons of Zilpah, Leah’s handmaid: Gad and Asher. These are the sons of Jacob who were born to him in Mesopotamia of Syria. He also came to Isaac his father in Mamre, the city of Arba—this is Hebron—in which Abraham and Isaac had stayed. And the days of Isaac were completed, one hundred and eighty years; and being consumed by age, he died and was gathered to his people, an old man and full of days; and Esau and Jacob, his sons, buried him. **Chapter 36.**

**T**hese now are the generations of Esau; he is Edom. Esau took wives from the daughters of Canaan: Adah, the daughter of Elon the Hittite, and Oholibamah, the daughter of Anah, the daughter of Zibeon the Hivite. Also Basemath, the daughter of Ishmael, the sister of Nebaioth. Now Adah bore Eliphaz. Basemath gave birth to Reuel. Oholibamah gave birth to Jeush, and Jalam, and Korah. These are the sons of Esau who were born to him in the land of Canaan. And Esau took his wives, and sons, and daughters, and every soul of his house, and his substance, and cattle, and everything he was able to have in the land of Canaan, and went into another region; and he departed from his brother Jacob. For they were very rich and could not dwell together, nor could the land of their pilgrimage support them because of the multitude of their flocks. And Esau lived on Mount Seir; he is Edom. These now are the generations of Esau, the father of the Edomites, on Mount Seir, and these are the names of his sons: Eliphaz, the son of Adah, the wife of Esau; and Reuel, the son of Basemath, his wife. And the sons of Eliphaz were: Teman, Omar, Zepho, and Gatham.

<summary>This page covers the pivotal transition of Jacob being renamed Israel by God and the birth of his final son, Benjamin, alongside the death of his beloved wife Rachel. It concludes with the death of Isaac and the divergence of the lineages of Jacob and Esau, marking the beginning of the Edomite genealogy.</summary>

His brothers replied to him: "Shall you be our king? Or shall we be subjected to your dominion?" Therefore, this matter of the dreams and his words provided fuel for envy and hatred. He also saw another dream, which he told to his brothers, saying: "I saw in a dream as if the sun and the moon and eleven stars were bowing down to me." When he had related this to his father and his brothers, his father rebuked him and said: "What does this dream that you have seen mean? Shall I and your mother and your brothers bow down to you upon the earth?" His brothers therefore envied him; but his father considered the matter in silence. And when those brothers were staying in Shechem <note>original: "sichem"</note> to pasture their father's flocks, Israel <note>original: "isrl'"</note> said to him: "Your brothers are pasturing the sheep in Shechem; come, I will send you to them." When Joseph replied, "I am ready," Israel said: "Go and see if all is well <note>original: "prospera sint"</note> with your brothers and the livestock, and bring word back to me as to what is happening." Being sent from the valley of Hebron, he came to Shechem. And a man found him wandering in the field and asked what he was seeking. But he replied: "I am seeking my brothers. Tell me where they are pasturing the flocks." And the man said to him: "They have moved from this place. I heard them saying: 'Let us go to Dothan' <note>original: "dothaim"</note>." Joseph therefore went after his brothers and found them in Dothan. When they saw him from afar, before he approached them, they plotted to kill him, and they spoke to one another: "Behold, the dreamer comes. Come, let us kill him and throw him into an old cistern <note>original: "cisternam veterem"</note>; and we shall say: 'A wild beast has devoured him'; and then it will appear what his dreams profit him." But Reuben, hearing this, strove to deliver him from their hands and said: "Let us not kill him; do not shed blood, but cast him into this old cistern which is in the wilderness, and keep your hands harmless." He said this wanting to rescue him from their hands and return him to his father. Immediately, therefore, as Joseph came to his brothers, they stripped him of his long-sleeved and multi-colored tunic <note>original: "tunica talari et polimita," often translated as the 'coat of many colors'</note> and cast him into the old cistern, which had no water. And sitting down to eat bread, they saw Ishmaelite travelers coming from Gilead, and their camels carrying spices, resin, and myrrh <note>original: "stacten"</note> into Egypt. Judah therefore said to his brothers: "What does it profit us if we kill our brother and conceal his blood? It is better that he be sold to the Ishmaelites, and our hands not be defiled. For he is our brother and our flesh." They agreed to his words. And as the Midianite merchants were passing by, they drew Joseph out of the cistern and sold him to the Ishmaelites for twenty pieces of silver; and they led him into Egypt. And Reuben, returning to the cistern, did not find the boy; and with his clothes torn, he went to his brothers and said: "The boy is gone; and I, where shall I go?" But they took his tunic and dipped it in the blood of a goat which they had killed, sending people to carry it to their father and say: "We found this; see whether it is the tunic of your son or not." When the father recognized it, he said: "It is the tunic of my son. A wild beast has eaten him; a beast has devoured Joseph." And with his garments torn, he put on sackcloth <note>original: "cilicio"</note>, mourning his son for a long time. And when all his children gathered to ease the father's grief, he would not accept consolation and said: "I shall go down to my son in the underworld <note>original: "infernum," here referring to the grave or the place of the dead</note> mourning." And as he continued in weeping, the Midianites sold Joseph in Egypt to Potiphar, a eunuch <note>original: "eunucho," here meaning a high-ranking official or chamberlain</note> of Pharaoh and the captain of the soldiers.

And Joseph found grace in the presence of his master and served him. By whom he was set over all things, governing the house entrusted to him and everything that had been delivered to him. Therefore after many days, his mistress cast her eyes upon Joseph and said, "Sleep with me." But he, by no means consenting to the wicked deed, said to her: "Behold, my master, having delivered everything to me, does not know what he has in his own house, nor is there anything which is not in my power or that he has not delivered to me, except you who are his wife. How then can I do this evil and sin against my lord <note>The Latin 'dominum meum' can mean both 'my master' and 'my Lord [God]'; Joseph likely refers to the betrayal of his master's trust and his duty to God.</note>?" She spoke words of this kind every single day, and the woman was troublesome to the young man, but he refused the debauchery.

It happened, however, on a certain day that Joseph entered the house to do some work without any witnesses, and she, having caught the hem of his garment, said: "Sleep with me." He entered to me to lie with me, and when I cried out and he heard my voice, he left the cloak I was holding and fled outside." Therefore, as proof of her story <note>original: 'fidei', literally 'faith' or 'credibility'</note>, she kept the cloak and showed it to her husband when he returned home,

and she said: "The Hebrew servant whom you brought entered to me to mock me. And when he saw me cry out, he left the cloak I was holding and fled outside." Having heard these things, the master, being too ready to believe his wife's words, was very angry and delivered Joseph into the prison where the king's prisoners were kept, and he was shut up there. But the Lord was with Joseph and had mercy on him, giving him grace in the sight of the chief of the prison. Who delivered into his hand all the prisoners who were held in custody, and whatever was done was under him; nor did the keeper know anything of the affairs entrusted to him. For the Lord was with him, and directed all his works.

### **Chapter 40.**

**S**o after these things were done, it happened that two eunuchs <term>eunuchs: in this context, the term refers to high-ranking court officials or chamberlains</term>—the cupbearer <term>cupbearer: an official who tastes and serves wine for the king to ensure its quality and safety</term> of the king of Egypt and the baker—offended their lord. When Joseph had entered to them in the morning and saw them sad, he inquired saying: "Why is your face sadder today than usual?" The chief of the cupbearers told his dream first: "I saw before me a vine on which were three branches, growing little by little into buds, and after the flowers, the grapes ripened; and Pharaoh's cup was in my hand. Seeing that he had interpreted the dream so wisely, the master of the bakers said: "I also saw a dream—that I had three baskets of flour upon my head, and in the one basket which was highest, I thought I carried all the foods made by the baker’s art, and the birds ate from it." Joseph answered: "This is the interpretation of the dream. The three baskets are three days yet to come, after which Pharaoh will take away your head and hang you on a gallows <note>original: 'cruce' - while literally 'cross,' in this context it refers to a tree or gallows for public display of the body</note>, and the birds shall tear at your flesh." 

Then, the third day was Pharaoh's birthday. While making a great feast for his servants, he remembered the master of the cupbearers and the chief of the bakers amidst the banquet. He restored the one to his place to reach out the cup to him, and the other he hung on a gallows, so that the truth of the interpreter might be proven. And yet, as prosperity followed him, the chief of the cupbearers forgot his interpreter.

## <insert>Chapter 41</insert>

**A**fter two years, Pharaoh saw a dream. He thought he stood above the river, from which ascended seven cows, beautiful and very fat, and they fed in marshy places. And seven others emerged from the river, foul and wasted with leanness, and they fed on the very bank of the river in the greenest and most fertile places. These devoured the cows whose appearance and habit of body had been so marvelous. Pharaoh, having awakened, slept again and saw another dream. Seven ears of grain sprouted on a single stalk, full 

---

and handsome; and as many other thin ears, struck with blight <note>original: 'uredine' - a scorching wind or fungal disease that shrivels crops</note>, arose, devouring all the beauty of the former ones. 

Pharaoh, waking after his rest and being struck with terror when morning came, sent to all the diviners <term>coniectores: those who interpret signs or dreams by 'throwing together' symbols and meanings</term> of Egypt and all the wise men. Having called them, he told them the dream, but there was no one who could interpret it. Then at last, the master of the cupbearers, remembering, said: "I confess my sin. The king, angry with his servants, ordered me and the master of the bakers to be thrust into the prison of the captain of the soldiers, where in one night we both saw a dream that was a foreboding of future things. There was a Hebrew youth there, a servant of that same leader of the soldiers. When we told him our dreams, we heard exactly what the event later proved true. For I was restored to my office, and the other was hung on a gallows." 

Immediately, at the king's command, they brought Joseph out of prison and shaved him; and having changed his clothes, they presented him to Pharaoh. The king said to him: "I have seen dreams, and there is no one who can explain them; I have heard that you interpret most wisely." Joseph answered: "Without me, God will give a prosperous answer to Pharaoh." 

Pharaoh therefore told what he had seen: "I thought I stood upon the bank of the river, and seven cows came up from the river, very beautiful and with fat flesh, which cropped the greenery in the marshy pasture. And behold, there followed these seven other cows, so deformed and lean that I never saw the like in all the land of Egypt. These, having devoured and consumed the former ones, gave no sign of being full, but remained sluggish with the same leanness and filth. Awakening, and then again weighed down by sleep, I saw a dream. Seven ears of grain sprouted on one stalk, full and most beautiful. And seven other thin ones, struck with blight, arose from the straw, which devoured the beauty of the former. I told the dream to the diviners, and there is no one who can explain it." Previously, Joseph had been summoned from prison to interpret the troubling dreams of Pharaoh regarding seven fat and seven thin cows, and seven full and seven withered ears of grain. This page records Joseph’s explanation of the dreams and his subsequent rise to power as the vizier of Egypt.</meta>

# Genesis.

...of the king. It is one and the same. **W**hat God is about to do, he has revealed to Pharaoh. **T**he seven beautiful cows and seven full ears of grain are seven years of plenty: they share the same meaning in the dream. **T**he seven thin and sickly cows that came up after them, and the seven thin ears of grain blasted by the scorching wind, are the seven years of famine to come, which shall be fulfilled in this order. **B**ehold, seven years of great fertility shall come into the whole land of Egypt; which will be followed by another seven years of such great barrenness that all the previous abundance will be given over to oblivion. **F**or the famine shall consume the entire land, and the greatness of the scarcity shall destroy the magnitude of the harvest's plenty. **A**s for the fact that you saw the dream a second time pertaining to the same matter, it is a sign of its certainty: because the word of God is established and shall be fulfilled more swiftly. **N**ow therefore, let the king provide a wise and industrious man, and set him over the land of Egypt; let him appoint overseers throughout every region, and let a fifth part of the crops—through the seven years of fertility which are now to come—be gathered into granaries, and all the grain be stored under Pharaoh’s power and kept in the cities. **L**et it be prepared for the future seven years of famine which are to overwhelm Egypt, so that the land may not be consumed by want. This counsel pleased Pharaoh and all his ministers. **H**e spoke to them: "Could we find such a man who is so full of the spirit of God?" **T**herefore, he said to Joseph: "Since God has shown you all these things you have spoken, could I find someone wiser or more like you? **Y**ou shall be over my house, and all my people shall obey the command of your mouth. Only in the throne of the kingdom will I precede you." **A**nd Pharaoh said again to Joseph: "**B**ehold, I have appointed you over the whole land of Egypt." **H**e took the ring from his hand and gave it into Joseph’s hand; he clothed him in a robe of fine linen <note>original: 'bissina' — referring to a high-quality, fine cloth, likely linen or silk used by royalty</note>,

<column-break/>

and placed a gold collar <note>original: 'torquem' — a twisted metal necklace or collar, a symbol of high status or office</note> around his neck. **H**e made him ascend upon his second chariot, with a herald crying out that everyone should bow their knee before him and know that he was appointed over the entire land of Egypt. The king of Egypt also said to Joseph: "**I** am Pharaoh. **W**ithout your command, no one shall move hand or foot in all the land of Egypt." **A**nd he changed his name, calling him in the Egyptian tongue the "Savior of the World" <note>original: 'saluatorem mūdi' — In the Hebrew Bible, the name given is 'Zaphnath-Paaneah.' The Latin text uses a translation that reflects a traditional Christian interpretation of Joseph as a 'type' or precursor to Christ.</note>. **H**e gave him as a wife Aseneth, the daughter of Potipherah <note>original: 'phutifaris'</note>, the priest of Heliopolis. **S**o Joseph went out into the land of Egypt. He was thirty years old when he stood in the presence of King Pharaoh, and he traveled through all the regions of Egypt. **T**he fertility of the seven years arrived, and the grain, gathered into sheaves, was collected into the granaries of Egypt. **E**very abundance of the harvest was stored in the individual cities. The abundance of wheat was so great that it was equal to the sands of the sea, and the quantity exceeded measurement. **T**wo sons were born to Joseph before the famine came, whom Aseneth, daughter of Potipherah the priest of Heliopolis, bore to him. **H**e called the name of the firstborn Manasseh, saying: "God has made me forget all my labors and my father’s house." **T**he name of the second he called Ephraim, saying: "**G**od has made me grow in the land of my poverty." **T**herefore, the seven years of abundance which had been in Egypt passed, and the seven years of scarcity began to come, which Joseph had predicted. A famine prevailed in the whole world. **B**ut in all the land of Egypt, there was famine. **W**hen they were hungry, the people cried out to Pharaoh, asking for food. **H**e answered them: "Go to Joseph, and do whatever he tells you." **T**he famine grew daily in every land, and Joseph opened all the granaries and sold to the Egyptians. For the famine had overwhelmed them as well.

said to his sons: "Why are you neglectful?
I have heard that wheat is being sold in Egypt.
Go down and buy for us what is necessary,
so that we may live and not be consumed
by want." Therefore, ten of Joseph's brothers
went down to buy grain in Egypt,
but Benjamin was kept at home by Jacob, who
had said to his brothers: "Lest perhaps he might
suffer some evil on the journey." They entered the land
of Egypt with others who were traveling to buy.
Now there was a famine in the land of Canaan, and
Joseph was the governor <term>princeps</term> <note>While the text says "princeps" (prince/leader), it refers to his role as the Vizier or chief administrator of Egypt.</note> in the land of Egypt; and
at his command grain was sold to the people.
And when his brothers had bowed down to him and
he had recognized them, he spoke to them
harshly, as if to strangers, questioning them: "Where
have you come from?" They answered: "From the land of Canaan,
to buy the necessities of life." And though he
knew his brothers, he was not recognized by
them. Remembering the dreams he had once
seen, he said to them: "You are spies;
you have come to see the weaker parts <note>original: "infirmiora terre" - literally the 'infirmities' or 'unfortified areas' of the land.</note> of the land."
They said: "It is not so, lord; but your servants
have come to buy food. We are all the sons
of one man. We have come as peaceful men;
your servants plot no evil."
To which he replied: "It is otherwise. You have
come to consider the unfortified areas of this place." But they
said: "We your servants are twelve brothers,
sons of one man in the land of Canaan.
The youngest is with our father; the other is no more."
"This is what I have said," he replied. "You are
spies. Now I shall take a test of you.
By the health <note>original: "Per salutem pharaonis" - an oath asserting the king's life or safety as a guarantee of truth.</note> of Pharaoh, you shall not leave
from here until your youngest brother comes.
Send one of you to bring him;
but you shall be in chains, until those things
you have said are proven, whether they be true or false."

<column-break/>

"Otherwise, by the health of Pharaoh,
you are spies." He therefore delivered them
into custody for three days. On the third day,
having brought them out of prison, he said: "Do what I have said
and you shall live. For I fear God. If you are
peaceful men, let one of your brothers be bound in prison;
but as for you, go and carry the grain you have bought
to your homes, and bring your youngest brother
to me, so that I may be able to prove
your words and you shall not die." They did

rightly suffer these things, because we sinned against
our brother, seeing the anguish of his soul
while he pleaded with us, and we did not listen;
therefore this tribulation has come upon us."
Among them, Reuben said: "Did I not
say to you, 'do not sin against the boy,' and
you would not listen to me? Behold, his blood
is required." They did not know that
Joseph understood them, because he spoke to
them through an interpreter. He turned
away for a moment and wept; and having
returned, he spoke to them, taking
Simeon and binding him in their presence. He ordered

their bags, besides giving them provisions for the

When one opened his sack to give fodder to his beast
at the inn, he saw the money in the mouth
of the bag, and said to his brothers: "My money
has been returned to me. Look, it is in the sack." And
they were struck with amazement and troubled, saying to one another:
"What is this that God has done to us?"
And they came to Jacob their father in the land

spies of the province. To whom we answered: 'We are
peaceful men, and we do not plot any treachery.
We are twelve brothers born of one father;
one is no more; the youngest is with our father...'"

After his father was buried, Joseph’s brothers—fearing his reaction and speaking among themselves—said, "Perhaps he remembers the injury he suffered and will repay us for all the evil we did to him." They sent word to him, saying: "Your father commanded us before he died that we should say these things to you: 'I beseech you to forget the crime of your brothers and the sin and malice they practiced against you.' We also pray that you forgive this iniquity for the servants of the God of your father." 

Hearing these words, Joseph wept. His brothers then came to him, prostrated themselves on the ground, and said, "We are your servants." He answered them: "Do not be afraid. Can we resist the will of God? You intended evil against me, but God turned it into good, so that He might exalt me as you see at present, and save many peoples. Do not fear. I will feed you and your little ones." And he comforted them, speaking gently and mildly. 

Joseph lived in Egypt with all his father's household. He lived one hundred and ten years and saw the children of Ephraim unto the third generation. The sons of Machir, the son of Manasseh, were born on Joseph's knees <note>Being "born on the knees" is an ancient symbolic gesture of adoption and legal recognition.</note>. And when he had bound them by an oath, saying, "God will visit you; carry my bones with you from this place," he died, having completed one hundred and ten years of his life. Embalmed with spices, he was placed in a coffin <note>original: "loculo" — literally a small place or box; here referring to a sarcophagus or casket.</note> in Egypt.

# Here begins the book of Hellesmoth: which we call Exodus.
<note>original: "hellesmoth" — This is a Latin transliteration of the Hebrew title "Ve-elleh shemot," meaning "And these are the names."</note>

**H**e <note>The letter 'H' is a large, gold-illuminated initial in the original manuscript, marking the start of a new book of the Bible.</note> are the names of the sons of Israel who entered Egypt with Jacob. Each entered with his own household: Reuben, Simeon, Levi, Judah, Issachar, Zebulun, and Benjamin, Dan and Naphtali, Gad and Asher. Therefore, all the souls of those who came from the thigh of Jacob were seventy. Joseph, however, was already in Egypt. After he died, along with all his brothers and that entire generation, the children of Israel grew; and as if sprouting, they multiplied and, becoming exceedingly strong, they filled the land.

<margin>whenever</margin> <note>original: "qunque" — likely an abbreviation for "quandoque," often used in margins to mark the beginning of a specific liturgical reading.</note>
Meanwhile, a new king arose over Egypt who did not know Joseph. And he said to his people: "Behold, the people of the children of Israel are many, and stronger than us. Come, let us wisely oppress them, lest they multiply; for if war breaks out against us, they might join our enemies, fight against us, and depart from the land." 

Therefore, he set masters of the works <note>Taskmasters or labor bosses.</note> over them to afflict them with burdens. And they built for Pharaoh the cities of storehouses: Pithom and Rameses. But the more they oppressed them, the more they multiplied and grew. The Egyptians hated the children of Israel and afflicted them, mocking and envying them. They led their lives into bitterness through hard labor with clay and bricks, and with all the service by which they were crushed in the labors of the earth.

Moreover, the king of Egypt spoke to the midwives of the Hebrews, one of whom was called Shiphrah <note>original: "sephora"</note> and the other Puah <note>original: "phua"</note>, commanding them: "When you act as midwives for the Hebrew women and the time of birth arrives, if it is a male, kill him; if it is a female, keep her." But the midwives feared God and did not do as the king of Egypt commanded, but preserved the males. 

When they were summoned to him, the king said: "What is this that you have done, that you have saved the boys?" They answered: "The Hebrew women are not like the Egyptian women. For they themselves have the knowledge of midwifery, and they give birth before we come to them." Therefore, the Lord did well for the midwives; and the people grew and became exceedingly strong.

**Moses.** "I will go and see this great sight: why the bush is not burnt." But when the Lord saw that he went forward to see: he called him from the middle of the bush, and said: "Moses, Moses." To whom the Lord said: "I have seen the affliction of my people in Egypt: and I have heard their cry: because of the hardness <note>original: "duriciam"; referring to the cruelty or harshness</note> of those who are in charge of the works.
