And Moses said to God: "Who am I, that I should go to Pharaoh: and lead the children of Israel out of Egypt?" Who said to him: "I will be with you: and you shall have this sign that I have sent you. When you have led my people out of Egypt: you shall sacrifice to God upon this mountain." Moses said to God: "Behold, I shall go to the children of Israel and say to them: 'The God of your fathers has sent me to you.' If they should say to me, 'What is his name?': what shall I say to them?" The Lord said to Moses: **"I am who I am."** <note>original: "Ego sum qui sum." This is the foundational revelation of the Divine Name, often associated with the name Yahweh.</note> He said: "Thus you shall say to the children of Israel: '**He Who Is**' <note>original: "Qui est"</note> has sent me to you." 

And God said again to Moses: "These things you shall say to the children of Israel: 'The Lord God of your fathers, the God of Abraham, and the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob, has sent me to you.' This is my name forever: and this is my memorial <note>A name to be remembered by</note> from generation to generation. Go and gather the elders of Israel: and you shall say to them: 'The Lord God of your fathers appeared to me: the God of Abraham, and the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob, saying: Visiting, I have visited you <note>A Hebrew idiom meaning "I have surely/certainly visited you"</note>: and I have seen all that has happened to you in Egypt: and I have said that I will lead you out of the affliction of Egypt into the land of the Canaanite, and the Hittite, and the Amorite, and the Perizzite, and the Hivite, and the Jebusite, to a land flowing with milk and honey': and they shall hear your voice. And you and the elders of Israel shall enter to the king of Egypt: and you shall say to him: 'The Lord God of the Hebrews has called us. We will go a three days' journey into the wilderness: so that we may sacrifice to the Lord our God.' 

But I know that the king of Egypt will not let you go: except by a strong hand. And I will stretch out my hand and strike Egypt with all my wonders which I am about to do in their midst. After these things he will let you go: and I will give favor to this people before the Egyptians. And when you depart, you shall not go out empty: but every woman shall ask from her neighbor and from her guest: silver and gold vessels, and clothing: and you shall put them upon your sons and your daughters: and you shall despoil Egypt." 

**Chapter 4.**

**R**esponding, <note>The large blue initial 'R' marks the start of the new chapter.</note> Moses said: "They will not believe me: nor will they hear my voice: but they will say: 'The Lord has not appeared to you.'" Therefore he said to him: "What is that which you hold in your hand?" He responded: "**A rod.**" <note>original: "Virga"; a shepherd’s staff or walking stick.</note> And the Lord said: "Throw it on the ground." He threw it: and it was turned into a serpent: so that Moses fled from it. And the Lord said: "Stretch out your hand: and catch its tail." He stretched it out and held it: and it was turned into a rod. "So that they may believe," he said, "that the God of your fathers has appeared to you: the God of Abraham, and the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob." 

And the Lord said again: "Put your hand into your bosom." When he had put it into his bosom: he brought it out leprous, like snow. "Draw back your hand," he said, "into your bosom." He drew it back and brought it out again: and it was like the rest of his flesh.

<summary>This page depicts the pivotal moment of the Burning Bush, where God reveals His name ("I AM WHO I AM") to Moses and commissions him to lead Israel out of Egypt. It also describes the first miraculous signs given to Moses—the rod turning into a serpent and his hand becoming leprous—to prove his divine authority.</summary>

Moses said: "I beseech you, Lord, I am not eloquent since yesterday or the day before <note>original: "nudiustertius"; a Latin idiom meaning "the day before yesterday," used here to mean "previously"</note>: and since you have spoken to your servant, I am of a more hindered and slower tongue." **T**he Lord said to him: "Who made the mouth of man? # Exodus.

...you shall say: "You shall no longer give straw to the people for making bricks as before; but let them go and gather stubble <note>original: "stipulas"; refers to the stalks of grain left in the field after harvest</note> for themselves. And the measure of bricks which they were making before, you shall impose upon them; you shall not diminish anything. For they are idle <note>original: "vacant"; meaning they have too much free time</note>, and therefore they cry out, saying: 'Let us go and sacrifice to our God.' Let them be oppressed with work, and let them fulfill it, so that they do not give heed to lying words."

Therefore the overseers of the works and the taskmasters went out and said to the people: "Thus says Pharaoh: I do not give you straw. Go and gather it wherever you can find it; nor shall anything of your work be diminished." And the people were dispersed throughout all the land of Egypt to gather straw. And the overseers of the works were urgent, saying: "Complete your daily work, as you were accustomed to do before when straw was given to you." 

And those from the children of Israel who were in charge of the works were beaten by Pharaoh’s taskmasters, saying: "Why have you not fulfilled the measure of bricks as before, neither yesterday nor today?" And the officers of the children of Israel came and cried out to Pharaoh, saying: "Why do you act this way against your servants? Straw is not given to us, and bricks are likewise demanded of us. Behold, we your servants are beaten with whips, and injustice is done against your people." 

But he said: "You are idle and lazy <note>original: "vacatis ocio"; literally "you are empty of business," a harsh accusation of laziness</note>, and therefore you say: 'Let us go and sacrifice to the Lord.' Go therefore and work. Straw shall not be given to you, and you shall return the customary number of bricks." And the officers of the children of Israel saw that they were in a bad state, because it was said to them: "Nothing of the work of the bricks shall be diminished for each day." 

And they met Moses and Aaron, who were standing opposite them as they came out from Pharaoh. And they said to them: "May the Lord see and judge, for you have made our odor to stink <note>original: "fetere fecistis odorem nostrum"; a common ancient metaphor for making someone loathsome or hated</note> before Pharaoh and his servants, and you have provided him a sword to kill us." And Moses returned to the Lord and said:

---

"Lord, why have you afflicted this people? Why did you send me? For from the time I went in to Pharaoh to speak in your name, he has afflicted your people, and you have not delivered them."

**A**nd the Lord said to Moses: **Chapter VI.** "Now you shall see what I am going to do to Pharaoh. For by a strong hand he shall let them go, and with a mighty hand he shall cast them out of his land." And the Lord spoke to Moses, saying: "I am the Lord who appeared to Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob as God Almighty <note>original: "deo omnipotente"; translating the Hebrew "El Shaddai"</note>: and my name Adonai <note>original: "adonay"; a title used by the Israelites to avoid speaking the sacred four-letter name of God aloud</note> I did not reveal to them. 

And I established my covenant with them to give them the land of Canaan, the land of their pilgrimage in which they were strangers. I have heard the groaning of the children of Israel, with which the Egyptians have oppressed them: and I have remembered my covenant. Therefore say to the children of Israel: I am the Lord, who will lead you out from the workhouse <note>original: "ergastulo"; a term for a prison-like factory for slaves</note> of the Egyptians, and will rescue you from servitude, and will redeem you with an outstretched arm and great judgments. 

And I will take you to myself for a people, and I will be your God. And you shall know that I am the Lord your God, who brought you out of the workhouse of the Egyptians. And I will lead you into the land over which I lifted my hand <note>original: "leuaui manum meam"; a gesture of taking a solemn oath</note> to give it to Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob: and I will give it to you to possess, I am the Lord." 

Moses therefore told all these things to the children of Israel; but they did not listen to him because of their anguish of spirit and most cruel labor. And the Lord spoke to Moses, saying: "Go in and speak to Pharaoh, king of Egypt, that he may let the children of Israel go out of his land." Moses answered before the Lord: "Behold, the children of Israel do not hear me; and how will Pharaoh hear me, especially since I am of uncircumcised lips? <note>original: "incircumcisus sim labijs"; a Hebrew idiom meaning Moses feels he lacks the eloquence or the right "fitness" to speak to a king</note>" 

And the Lord spoke to Moses and Aaron, and gave a command to the children of Israel and to Pharaoh, king of Egypt, that they should lead the children of Israel out of the land of Egypt. These are the heads of the houses by their families. The sons of Reuben, the firstborn of Israel:

# Exodus.

...Enoch and Pallu, Hezron and Carmi. These are the families of Reuben. The sons of Simeon: Jemuel, Jamin, Ohad, Jachin, Zohar, and Shaul, the son of a Canaanite woman. These are the generations of Simeon. And these are the names of the sons of Levi according to their genealogies: Gershon, Kohath, and Merari. Moreover, the years of Levi’s life were one hundred and thirty-seven. The sons of Gershon: Libni and Shimei, according to their families. The sons of Kohath: Amram, Izhar, Hebron, and Uzziel. Also, the years of Kohath’s life were one hundred and thirty-three. The sons of Merari: Mahli and Mushi. These are the families of Levi according to their households. 

Amram took his aunt <note>original: "patruelem"; refers to a father's sister</note> Jochebed as his wife, who bore him Aaron, Moses, and Miriam. And the years of Amram’s life were one hundred and thirty-seven. Also, the sons of Izhar: Korah, Nepheg, and Zichri. Also, the sons of Uzziel: Mishael, Elzaphan, and Sithri. Aaron took as his wife Elisheba, the daughter of Amminadab and sister of Nahshon, who bore him Nadab, Abihu, Eleazar, and Ithamar. Also, the sons of Korah: Assir, Elkanah, and Abiasaph. These are the families of the Korahites. But Eleazar, the son of Aaron, took a wife from the daughters of Putiel, who bore him Phinehas. These are the heads of the Levite households according to their families. 

This is the same Aaron and Moses to whom the Lord commanded: "Lead the children of Israel out of the land of Egypt by their ranks." These are the ones who spoke to Pharaoh, king of Egypt, to lead the children of Israel out of Egypt. This is that Moses and Aaron, on the day when the Lord spoke to Moses in the land of Egypt. And the Lord spoke to Moses, saying: "I am the Lord. Speak to Pharaoh, king of Egypt, everything that I say to you." And Moses said before the Lord: "Behold, I am of uncircumcised lips <note>original: "incircuncisus labijs"; a Hebrew idiom indicating that Moses felt he was a clumsy or unskilled speaker</note>. **A**nd the Lord said to Moses: "Behold, I have appointed you as a god <note>original: "deum"; here meaning one who acts with divine authority and power over a mortal king</note> to Pharaoh, and Aaron your brother shall be your prophet. 

---

You shall speak to him all that I command you, and he shall speak to Pharaoh, so that he may let the children of Israel go from his land. And I will lay my hand upon Egypt and lead out my army and my people, the children of Israel, from the land of Egypt by great judgments. And the Egyptians shall know that I am the Lord, when I have stretched out my hand over Egypt and led the children of Israel out from their midst." 

Therefore, Moses and Aaron did just as the Lord had commanded them; so they did. Now Moses was eighty years old and Aaron was eighty-three when they spoke to Pharaoh. And the Lord said to Moses and Aaron: "When Pharaoh says to you, 'Show us a sign,' you shall say to Aaron: 'Take your rod and cast it down before Pharaoh, and it shall be turned into a serpent <note>original: "colubrum"; a general term for a snake or serpent</note>.'" 

So Moses and Aaron went in to Pharaoh and did as the Lord had commanded. Aaron took the rod before Pharaoh and his servants, and it was turned into a serpent. But Pharaoh called the wise men and the sorcerers, and they also did the same through Egyptian incantations and certain secrets. Each of them cast down their rods, and they were turned into dragons <note>original: "dracones"; in medieval Latin, this often referred to large serpents, though Aaron’s is earlier called a "colubrum"</note>. But Aaron’s rod swallowed their rods. And Pharaoh’s heart was hardened, and he did not listen to them, just as the Lord had commanded. 

Then the Lord said to Moses: "Pharaoh's heart has become heavy; he refuses to let the people go. Go to him in the morning; behold, he will go out to the water. And you shall stand to meet him on the bank of the river, and the rod which was turned into a dragon you shall take in your hand. And you shall say to him: 'The Lord God of the Hebrews has sent me to you, saying: Let my people go, that they may sacrifice to me in the desert; and behold, until now...'"

# Exodus.

...you would not listen to me. **Thus, therefore, says the Lord.** <note>original: "Hec igitur dicit dñs"</note> **In this you shall know that I am the Lord.** Behold, I will strike the water of the river with the rod that is in my hand, and it shall be turned into blood. The fish also that are in the river shall die, and the waters shall rot, and the Egyptians shall be distressed drinking the water of the river. **And the Lord said to Moses:** Say to Aaron: Take your rod, and stretch out your hand over the waters of Egypt—and over their rivers, and the streams and marshes, and all the pools of water—that they may be turned into blood; and let there be blood in all the land of Egypt, as well in vessels of wood as in those of stone. And Moses and Aaron did as the Lord had commanded. And lifting up the rod, he struck the water of the river before Pharaoh and his servants; and it was turned into blood. And the fish that were in the river died. And the river rotted, and the Egyptians could not drink the water of the river, and there was blood in all the land of Egypt. And the magicians of the Egyptians did in like manner with their enchantments. And Pharaoh’s heart was hardened, and he did not listen to them, as the Lord had commanded. And he turned himself away, and went into his house, neither did he apply his heart to it even this time. And all the Egyptians dug around the river for water to drink; for they could not drink of the water of the river. And seven days were completed after the Lord had struck the river.

# Chapter VIII.

**D**ixit <note>The large blue 'D' marks the beginning of the eighth chapter.</note> **And the Lord said to Moses:** Go in to Pharaoh, and you shall say to him: **Thus says the Lord.** Let my people go, that they may sacrifice to me. And the frogs shall enter into you, and your people, and all your servants. **And the Lord said to Moses:** Say to Aaron: Stretch forth your hand over the rivers and over the streams and the marshes, and bring forth frogs upon the land of Egypt. And Aaron stretched forth his hand over the waters of Egypt, and the frogs came up and covered the land of Egypt. And the magicians also did in like manner by their enchantments, and they brought forth frogs upon the land of Egypt. **And Pharaoh called Moses and Aaron, and said to them:** Pray to the Lord that he may take away the frogs from me and from my people, and I will let the people go to sacrifice to the Lord. **And Moses said to Pharaoh:** Set a time for me when I shall pray for you, and for your servants, and for your people, that the frogs may be driven away from you, and from your house, and from your servants, and from your people, and may remain only in the river. And he answered: Tomorrow. But he said: I will do according to your word, that you may know that there is none like the Lord our God. And the frogs shall depart from you, and from your house, and from your servants, and from your people, and they shall remain only in the river. And Moses and Aaron went out from Pharaoh; and Moses cried to the Lord regarding the promise of the frogs, which he had agreed with Pharaoh. And the Lord did according to the word of Moses; and the frogs died in the houses, in the villages, and in the fields. And they gathered them together into immense heaps, and the land rotted. And Pharaoh, seeing that rest was given, hardened his heart, and did not listen to them, as the Lord had commanded. **And the Lord said to Moses:** Say to Aaron: Stretch forth your rod and strike the dust of the earth, that there may be gnats <note>original: "scinifes"; the Latin refers to stinging swarms, commonly translated as gnats, lice, or mosquitoes</note> in the whole land of Egypt. And they did so. And Aaron stretched forth his hand, holding the rod, and he struck the dust of the earth, and there were gnats upon men and upon beasts. All the dust of the earth was turned into gnats through the whole land of Egypt. And they did in like manner...

# Exodus.

...the magicians [tried] by their enchantments to bring forth gnats, but they could not. The Lord also said to Moses: Arise early in the morning and stand before Pharaoh. For he will go out to the water. And you shall say to him: **Thus says the Lord: Let my people go, that they may sacrifice to me.** <note>original: "Hec dicit dñs. Dimitte populū meum: ut sacrificet michi"</note> But if you will not let them go, behold, I will send upon you, and upon your servants, and upon your people, and into your houses, every kind of fly; and the houses of the Egyptians shall be filled with flies of various kinds, as well as the entire land where they dwell. And on that day I will make the land of Goshen <note>Goshen was the region in Egypt where the Israelites lived; it remained untouched by several of the plagues.</note> wonderful, in which my people dwell, so that no flies shall be there; that you may know that I am the Lord in the midst of the earth. And I will put a division between my people and your people. Tomorrow this sign shall occur. And the Lord did so. And a very grievous swarm of flies came into the house of Pharaoh and the houses of his servants, and into all the land of Egypt; and the land was corrupted by flies of this kind. And Pharaoh called for Moses and Aaron, and said to them: Go, and sacrifice to your God in this land. And Moses said: It cannot be done so. For we shall sacrifice the abominations of the Egyptians <note>original: "Abominaciones enī egiptioꝝ"</note> <note>Moses refers to the livestock—such as cattle or sheep—which the Egyptians worshipped or held sacred. To sacrifice them publicly would be an "abomination" to the Egyptians and likely cause a riot.</note> to the Lord our God. If we kill those things which the Egyptians worship before their eyes, they will overwhelm us with stones. We will go a three days' journey into the wilderness; and we will sacrifice to the Lord our God, as he has commanded us. And Pharaoh’s heart was hardened, so that even this time he would not let the people go.

# Exodus.

...he did not listen to them, just as the Lord had spoken to Moses. The Lord also said to Moses: Rise up early in the morning and stand before Pharaoh, and you shall say to him: **Thus says the Lord, the God of the Hebrews: Let my people go, that they may sacrifice to me.** <note>original: "Hec dicit dominus deus hebreorum. Dimitte populum meum ut sacrificet michi"</note> For this time I will send all my plagues upon your heart, and upon your servants, and upon your people, so that you may know there is none like me in all the earth. For now, stretching out my hand, I will strike you and your people with pestilence, and you shall perish from the earth. But for this reason have I raised you up: that I may show my power in you, and that my name may be declared throughout all the earth. Do you still hold back my people, and will you not let them go? Behold, tomorrow at this very hour I will rain down an exceedingly great hail, such as has not been seen in Egypt from the day it was founded until this present time. Send therefore now, and gather your livestock and all that you have in the field; for upon all men and beasts, and all things that are found outside and not gathered from the fields, the hail shall fall, and they shall die. He among the servants of Pharaoh who feared the word of the Lord made his servants and livestock flee into the houses; but he who neglected the word of the Lord left his servants and livestock in the fields. And the Lord said to Moses: Stretch out your hand toward heaven, that there may be hail in the entire land of Egypt: upon men, and upon beasts, and upon every herb of the field in the land of Egypt. And Moses stretched out his rod toward heaven, and the Lord sent thunder and hail, and lightning running across the ground. And the Lord rained hail upon the land of Egypt; and hail and fire mixed together were driven along. It was of such great magnitude as had never appeared before in all the land of Egypt since that nation was founded. And throughout all the land of Egypt, the hail struck everything that was in the fields, from man even to beast; and the hail struck every herb of the field and shattered every tree of the region. Only in the land of Goshen, <note>The Latin text uses "iessen." Goshen was the fertile region where the Israelites lived, which was spared from the worst effects of the plagues.</note> where the children of Israel were, did the hail not fall. And Pharaoh sent and called Moses and Aaron, saying to them: I have sinned even now. The Lord is just; I and my people are wicked. Pray to the Lord that the thunder of God and the hail may cease, so that I may let you go, and that you may stay here no longer. Moses said: When I have gone out of the city, I will stretch out my palms to the Lord; and the thunder shall cease, and there shall be no more hail, so that you may know that the earth is the Lord’s. But I know that both you and your servants do not yet fear the Lord God. The flax and the barley were therefore damaged, because the barley was green and the flax was already in bud. But the wheat and the spelt <note>Spelt is an ancient type of hulled wheat; the Latin "far" refers to this hardy grain.</note> were not damaged, because they were late-ripening. And Moses, having gone out from Pharaoh and from the city, stretched out his hands to the Lord; and the thunder and hail ceased, and the rain no longer poured down upon the earth. But Pharaoh, seeing that the rain and the hail and the thunder had ceased, increased his sin; and his heart was hardened exceedingly, and he did not let the children of Israel go, just as the Lord had commanded through the hand of Moses.

Moses and Aaron therefore went in to Pharaoh and said: Thus says the Lord, the God of the Hebrews: How long will you refuse to submit to me? Let my people go, that they may sacrifice to me. And he turned himself and went out from Pharaoh. Do you not see that Egypt is ruined?" And they brought back Moses and Aaron to Pharaoh, who said to them: "Go, sacrifice to the Lord your God. Who are they that are to go?" And Pharaoh answered: "May the Lord be with you in the same way that I let you and your little ones go! <note>Pharaoh is being sarcastic here, implying that he has no intention of letting the children go and that God's protection is as unlikely as their release.</note> Who can doubt that you are planning something very wicked? It shall not be so; but go only you men and sacrifice to the Lord, for this is what you yourselves requested." And they were immediately cast out from Pharaoh’s presence. They ascended over the whole land of Egypt and settled in all the borders of the Egyptians in incalculable numbers; such as had never been before that time, nor shall be hereafter. And they covered the entire surface of the earth, wasting everything. Therefore, the grass of the earth was devoured, and whatever fruit was on the trees which the hail had left. ### Chapter XII.

<insert>D</insert>ixit <note>The large blue initial 'D' marks the start of the instructions for the Passover.</note> The Lord also said to Moses and Aaron in the land of Egypt: "This month shall be to you the beginning of months; it shall be the first in the months of the year. without blemish, a male of one year. And the whole multitude of the children of Israel shall sacrifice it in the evening, and they shall take of its blood and put it upon both side-posts and on the lintels <note>original: "supliminaribus"; the upper cross-beam of the doorframe.</note> of the houses in which they shall eat it. If anything remains, you shall burn it with fire. And thus you shall eat it: you shall gird your loins <note>This means tucking long robes into a belt to allow for unhindered running or walking.</note>, you shall have shoes on your feet, holding staves in your hands, and you shall eat in haste. Moreover, you shall have this day for a memorial, and you shall celebrate it as a solemnity to the Lord in your generations by an everlasting worship. Seven days you shall eat unleavened bread. On the first day there shall be no leaven in your houses. Whosoever eats leavened bread, that soul shall perish from Israel, from the first day until the seventh day. The first day shall be holy and solemn, and the seventh day shall be venerable with the same festivity. You shall do no work in them, except those things which belong to eating; and you shall observe the feast of unleavened bread. For on this very same day, I will lead your army out of the land of Egypt..."

# Exodus.

...you shall keep this day throughout your generations by a perpetual rite. **I**n the first month, on the fourteenth day of the month at evening, you shall eat unleavened bread <term>azima: bread made without yeast, symbolizing the haste of the departure from Egypt</term> until the twenty-first day of the same month at evening. **F**or seven days leaven shall not be found in your houses. Whoever eats anything leavened, that soul shall perish from the congregation of Israel, whether he be a stranger or a native of the land. **Y**ou shall eat nothing leavened; **I**n all your dwellings you shall eat unleavened bread. **M**oses then called all the elders of the children of Israel and said to them: "Go and take a lamb <note>original: "animal"; usually refers to a yearling lamb or goat</note> for your families, and sacrifice the Passover <term>phase: the Latin transliteration of the Hebrew 'Pesach,' meaning 'to pass over'</term>. **A**nd dip a bunch of hyssop <note>hyssop: a common aromatic herb used in biblical purification rituals</note> in the blood that is on the threshold, and sprinkle the lintel and both side posts with it. **L**et none of you go out the door of his house until morning. **F**or the Lord will pass through striking the Egyptians. **A**nd when he sees the blood on the lintel and on both side posts, he will pass over the door of the house and will not allow the destroyer to enter your houses and harm you. **K**eep this word as a law for you and your children forever. **A**nd when you enter the land which the Lord God will give you as he promised, you shall observe these ceremonies. **A**nd when your children say to you, 'What is this ritual?' you shall say to them: '**I**t is the victim of the Lord's passing <note>original: "victima transitus dñi"</note>, when he passed over the houses of the children of Israel in Egypt, striking the Egyptians and delivering our houses." **A**nd the people bowed down and worshiped. **A**nd the children of Israel went out and did as the Lord had commanded Moses and Aaron. **I**t came to pass at midnight: the Lord struck every firstborn in the land of Egypt, from the firstborn of Pharaoh who sat on his throne to the firstborn of the captive woman who was in prison, and all the firstborn of the cattle. **A**nd Pharaoh rose in the night, and all his servants, and all Egypt; and a great cry arose in Egypt. **F**or there was not a house where a dead man did not lie. **A**nd Pharaoh called Moses and Aaron in the night and said: "**A**rise, go out from my people, you and the children of Israel. **G**o and sacrifice to the Lord as you say. **T**ake your sheep and herds as you asked, and departing, bless me." **A**nd the Egyptians urged the people to leave the land quickly, saying: "We shall all die." **T**herefore the people took their dough before it was leavened, and tying it in their cloaks, placed it on their shoulders. **A**nd the children of Israel did as the Lord had commanded Moses, and they asked the Egyptians for vessels of silver and gold and many clothes. **T**he Lord moreover gave the people favor before the Egyptians so that they lent to them, and they despoiled the Egyptians. **A**nd the children of Israel set out from Rameses to Succoth, about six hundred thousand men on foot, besides children and women. **A**nd a mixed crowd <note>original: "vulgus promiscuum"; referring to non-Israelites who chose to leave Egypt with the Hebrews</note> without number went up with them, and sheep and herds and very many animals of various kinds. **A**nd they baked the dough which they had brought out of Egypt a while ago, and made unleavened cakes baked under ashes <note>original: "subcinericios panes azimos"; bread cooked directly in the embers because they lacked ovens or time</note>. **F**or they could not be leavened because the Egyptians urged them to go and would not allow them to make any delay, nor had they prepared any provisions. **N**ow the dwelling of the children of Israel while they stayed in Egypt was four hundred and thirty years. **W**hen these were completed, on that same day all the army of the Lord went out from the land of Egypt. ¶ **T**his is an observable night of the Lord <note>original: "nox ista obseruabilis"; a night of watching or vigil</note> when he led them out of the land of Egypt. **T**his all the children of Israel must observe in their generations. **A**nd the Lord said to Moses and Aaron: "**T**his is the ritual of the Passover. **N**o foreigner shall eat of it. **B**ut every slave bought for money shall be circumcised, and then he shall eat. **T**he stranger and the hired hand shall not eat of it. **I**t shall be eaten in one house; He shall be like a native of the land. But if anyone is not circumcised, he shall not eat of it. The same law shall apply to the native and to the settler who sojourns among you. And the angel of the Lord, who went before the camp of Israel, removed himself and went behind them; and with him the pillar of cloud, leaving the front <note>original: "pora," a contraction of "priora"; the forward positions</note>, stood behind their backs, between the camp of the Egyptians and the camp of Israel. **A**nd it was a dark cloud, yet illuminating the night, so that they could not approach one another for the whole time of the night. **A**nd when Moses had stretched out his hand over the sea, the Lord took it away, with a strong and burning wind blowing all night, and turned it into dry land. **A**nd the water was divided, and the children of Israel entered through the middle of the dry sea. **F**or the water was like a wall on their right and on their left. **A**nd the Egyptians, pursuing, entered after them: all the cavalry of Pharaoh, his chariots and horsemen, through the middle of the sea. **A**nd now the morning watch <term>morning watch: the final part of the night, just before dawn, roughly 2:00 AM to 6:00 AM</term> had arrived; and behold, the Lord, looking down upon the camp of the Egyptians through the pillar of fire and cloud, slew their army and overturned the wheels of the chariots, and they were carried into the deep. **A**nd the Egyptians said: "Let us flee from Israel, for the Lord fights for them against us." **A**nd the Lord said to Moses: "**S**tretch out your hand over the sea, so that the waters may return upon the Egyptians, upon their chariots and their horsemen." **A**nd when Moses had stretched out his hand toward the sea, it returned at first light to its former place.

### .Exodus.

...and as the Egyptians were fleeing, the waters rushed over them, and the Lord rolled them into the midst of the waves. The waters returned and covered the chariots and the horsemen of Pharaoh's entire army that had followed them into the sea; not so much as one of them survived. But the children of Israel proceeded through the middle of the dry sea, and the waters were like a wall to them on their right and on their left. And the Lord delivered Israel that day from the hand of the Egyptians. And they saw the Egyptians dead upon the seashore, and the great hand <note>original: "manum magnam"; a biblical metaphor for God's overwhelming power and intervention</note> which the Lord had exercised against them. And the people feared the Lord, and they believed the Lord and His servant Moses. <insert>Chapter 15</insert>
**T**hen Moses and the children of Israel sang this song to the Lord, and they said: "Let us sing to the Lord, for He is gloriously magnified; the horse and its rider He has cast into the sea. The Lord is my strength and my praise, and He has become my salvation. This is my God and I will glorify Him; the God of my father, and I will exalt Him. The Lord is like a warrior <note>original: "uir pugnator"; literally 'a man of war,' a common ancient title for God in his role as protector</note>; Almighty is His name. Pharaoh's chariots and his army He has cast into the sea; his chosen princes are submerged in the Red Sea. The depths have covered them; they went down into the deep like a stone. Your right hand, O Lord, is magnified in strength; Your right hand, O Lord, has struck the enemy. And in the multitude of Your glory, You have overthrown my adversaries. You sent Your wrath, which devoured them like stubble. And by the breath of Your fury <note>original: "spiritu furoris tui"; referring to the wind that moved the waters</note> the waters were gathered together. The flowing wave stood still; the depths were gathered in the midst of the sea. The enemy said: 'I will pursue and I will overtake; I will divide the spoils; my soul shall be satisfied. I will unsheathe my sword; my hand shall slay them.' Your spirit blew, and the sea covered them; they sank like lead in the mighty waters. Who is like unto You among the strong, O Lord? Who is like You, magnificent in holiness,

<column-break/>

terrible and praiseworthy, and doing wonders? You stretched out Your hand, and the earth devoured them. You have been the leader in Your mercy to the people whom You have redeemed. And You have carried them in Your strength to Your holy dwelling. The peoples went up and were angry <note>original: "irati sunt"; in this context, it suggests the surrounding nations were trembling or agitated by the news</note>; sorrows took hold of the inhabitants of Philistia. Then the princes of Edom were troubled; trembling took hold of the mighty men of Moab; all the inhabitants of Canaan became stiff with fear. Let dread and fear fall upon them by the greatness of Your arm. Let them become as motionless as a stone until Your people pass over, O Lord, until this people of Yours pass over, whom You have possessed. You shall bring them in and plant them on the mountain of Your inheritance, in Your most firm dwelling which You have wrought, O Lord. Your sanctuary, O Lord, which Your hands have established. The Lord shall reign forever and beyond. For the horseman Pharaoh entered with his chariots and horsemen into the sea, and the Lord brought back the waters of the sea upon them. But the children of Israel walked on dry land in the midst of it." Therefore Miriam <note>original: "maria"</note> the prophetess, the sister of Aaron, took a timbrel <note>original: "tympanum"; a handheld percussion instrument similar to a tambourine</note> in her hand, and all the women went out after her with timbrels and dances, for whom she led the song, saying: "Let us sing to the Lord, for He is gloriously magnified; the horse and its rider He has cast into the sea." Then Moses led Israel from the Red Sea, and they went out into the desert of Shur. And they traveled for three days through the wilderness and found no water. And they came to Marah, but they could not drink the waters of Marah because they were bitter. Wherefore he also gave the place a fitting name, calling it Marah, that is, 'bitterness.' And the people murmured against Moses, saying: "What shall we drink?" But he cried out to the Lord, who showed him a piece of wood, which when he had cast into the waters, they turned to sweetness...

<insert>Chapter 16</insert>

**A**nd setting out from Elim, the whole multitude of the children of Israel came into the desert of Sin <note>The Wilderness of Sin is located between Elim and Mount Sinai; it is distinct from the "Zin" desert mentioned later in the journey</note>: which is between Elim and Sinai: on the fifteenth day of the second month after they had departed from the land of Egypt. And the whole congregation of the children of Israel murmured against Moses and Aaron in the wilderness. And the children of Israel said to them: "Would that we had died by the hand of the Lord in the land of Egypt, when we sat over the meat pots and ate bread to the full. Why have you led us into this desert, to kill the whole multitude with hunger?"

Then the Lord said to Moses: "Behold, I will rain bread from heaven for you. Let the people go out and collect what is sufficient for each day: that I may test them, whether they will walk in my law or not. But on the sixth day let them prepare what they bring in: and let it be double what they were accustomed to collect for each day."

And Moses and Aaron said to all the children of Israel: "In the evening you shall know that the Lord led you out of the land of Egypt: and in the morning you shall see the glory of the Lord. For he has heard your murmuring against the Lord. But what are we, that you mutter against us?" And Moses said: "The Lord will give you meat to eat in the evening, and bread to the full in the morning: because he has heard your murmurings with which you murmured against him. For what are we? Your murmuring is not against us, but against the Lord."

Moses also said to Aaron: "Say to the whole congregation of the children of Israel: 'Draw near before the Lord. For he has heard your murmuring.'" And while Aaron spoke to the whole assembly of the children of Israel, they looked toward the wilderness. And behold, the glory of the Lord appeared in a cloud.

And the Lord spoke to Moses, saying: "I have heard the murmurings of the children of Israel. Speak to them: 'In the evening you shall eat meat, and in the morning you shall be filled with bread: and you shall know that I am the Lord your God.'" So it happened in the evening that quail flew up and covered the camp: and in the morning a dew lay around the camp. And when it had covered the surface of the earth, there appeared in the wilderness something small, as if beaten with a pestle <note>original: "pilo tusum"; describing the fine, granular texture of the substance</note>, in the likeness of frost upon the ground. When the children of Israel saw it, they said to one another: **Manhu: which signifies, "What is this?"** <note>The Hebrew phrase 'Man hu' is the traditional source for the word 'Manna'</note> For they did not know what it was. To whom Moses said: "This is the bread which the Lord has given you to eat. This is the word which the Lord has commanded you: Let each one collect from it as much as is sufficient to eat: a gomer <term>gomer: an ancient Hebrew unit of dry measure, roughly equivalent to 2 to 4 liters</term> for each head. According to the number of your souls who dwell in the tabernacle, so shall you take it."

The children of Israel did so: and they collected it, some more, some less. and they measured it by the measure of a gomer. He who had collected more did not have more; nor did he who had prepared less find less: but each gathered according to what they were able to eat. And Moses said to them: "Let no one leave any of it until morning." They did not listen to him: but some of them left it until morning: and it began to swarm with worms and it rotted. And Moses was angry with them. They collected it in the morning, each one, as much as was sufficient to eat. And when the sun grew hot, it melted. But on the sixth day they collected food...

On the next day, Moses sat to judge the people, who stood by Moses from morning until evening. When his kinsman saw all that he was doing for the people, he said: "What is this that you are doing among the people? Why do you sit alone, and all the people wait from morning until evening?" 

Moses answered him: "The people come to me seeking the judgment of God. And when any dispute occurs among them, they come to me so that I may judge between them and show them the precepts of God and his laws." 

But he said: "The thing you are doing is not good. You are wearing yourself out with foolish labor, both you and this people who are with you. This business is beyond your strength; you cannot sustain it alone. Now hear my words and advice, and God will be with you. You should represent the people before God, to refer the things said to him. You should show the people the ceremonies and the ritual of worship, and the way by which they should walk, and the work they must do. 

"But provide from among all the people wise men who fear God, in whom is truth and who hate greed. Appoint from them <term>captains of thousands<note>original: *tribunos*</note></term>, and <term>hundreds<note>original: *centuriones*</note></term>, and <term>fifties<note>original: *quinquagenarios*</note></term>, and <term>tens<note>original: *decanos*</note></term>, who may judge the people at all times. If you do this, you will fulfill the command of the Lord and be able to endure his instructions; and all these people will return to their homes in peace." 

Having heard these things, Moses did everything that he had suggested. And having chosen capable men...

**A**nd the Lord spoke all these words: I am the Lord your God: who led you out of the land of Egypt: out of the house of slavery. You shall have no strange gods before me. You shall not make for yourself a carved image: nor any likeness of what is in heaven above: and what is on the earth below: nor of those things which are in the waters under the earth. You shall not adore them nor worship them. I am the Lord your God, strong and <term>jealous<note>original: *zelotes*; in this context, it refers to God's "zeal" or "exclusivity," demanding total devotion.</note></term>: visiting the iniquity of the fathers upon the children: unto the third and fourth generation of those who hate me: and showing mercy unto thousands to those who love me: and keep my commandments. You shall not take the name of the Lord your God in vain. For the Lord will not hold him guiltless: who takes the name of the Lord his God for a hollow purpose.
Remember to keep the <term>Sabbath<note>original: *sabbatum*; from the Hebrew word for "rest" or "cease."</note></term> day holy. Six days you shall labor: and do all your works. But on the seventh day is the Sabbath of the Lord your God: you shall do no work in it: you, and your son and your daughter: your male servant and your female servant: your beast and the <term>sojourner<note>original: *aduena*; a foreigner or guest living within the community.</note></term> who is within your gates. For in six days God made heaven and earth and the sea and all things that are in them: and he rested on the seventh day. Therefore the Lord blessed the Sabbath day: and made it holy. Honor your father and your mother: so that you may be long-lived upon the land which the Lord your God will give to you. You shall not kill. You shall not commit adultery. You shall not steal. You shall not speak false witness against your neighbor. You shall not covet your neighbor’s house. Neither shall you desire his wife: nor his male servant, nor female servant, nor ox, nor donkey: nor anything that is his. **T**hese are the <term>judgments<note>original: *iudicia*; legal ordinances or case laws that follow the general moral principles of the Ten Commandments.</note></term> which you shall set before them. If you buy a Hebrew servant: he shall serve you for six years: in the seventh he shall go out free for nothing. With what kind of garment he entered: with such let him go out. If he has a wife: his wife shall also go out with him. If, however, his master has given him a wife: and she has borne sons and daughters: the woman and her children shall be her master’s: but he himself shall go out with his own garment. But if the servant should say: I love my master and my wife and children: I will not go out free: his master shall bring him to the <note>original: *dijs*; literally "gods," but in this legal context, it refers to the judges or magistrates representing divine authority.</note> judges: and he shall be brought to the door and the doorposts: and he shall bore through his ear with an awl: and he shall be his servant forever. If any man sells his daughter as a maidservant: she shall not go out as female slaves are accustomed to go out. If she is displeasing in the eyes of her master to whom she was delivered: he shall let her go: but he shall not have the power to sell her to a foreign people: if he has rejected her. But if he has betrothed her to his son: he shall do to her according to the custom of daughters. But if he takes another for him...

The text transitions from the moral imperatives of the Decalogue to the practical judicial statutes of the community.</meta>

<header>.Exodus.</header>

he shall provide for the girl's marriage and clothing: and he shall not deny the price of her modesty <note>This refers to the rights of a female servant betrothed to a master's son; her basic needs and status must be maintained.</note>. **S**i if he does not do these three things: she shall go out for free without payment of money. **Q**ui he who strikes a man intending to kill him: let him die the death. **Q**ui but he who did not lie in wait—but God delivered him into his hands: I will appoint for you a place to which he ought to flee <note>This establishes the "cities of refuge" where those who committed accidental manslaughter could find safety from blood vengeance.</note>. **S**i if anyone kills his neighbor on purpose—and through treachery: you shall pluck him even from my altar so that he may die. **Q**ui he who strikes his father or mother: let him die the death. **Q**ui he who curses his father or mother: let him die the death. **Q**ui he who steals a man—and sells him: if convicted of the crime, let him die the death. **S**i if men have quarreled and one strikes his neighbor with a stone or a fist—and he did not die but lies in bed: if he rises and walks outside upon his staff: he who struck him shall be innocent: provided however that he restores his lost labor—and the expenses for the doctors. **Q**ui he who strikes his male or female servant with a rod and they die in his hands: he shall be guilty of a crime. **S**i but if the servant survives for one or two days—he shall not be subject to the penalty: for it is his money <note>original: *pecunia illius*; the servant was legally considered the master's property, so the loss of the servant's labor was seen as the master's own financial punishment.</note>. **S**i if men have quarreled—and someone strikes a pregnant woman and she miscarries—but she herself survives: he shall be subject to a fine as much as the woman's husband demands: and as the judges determine. **S**i but if her death follows: he shall give life for life. **O**culū eye for eye—tooth for tooth: hand for hand—foot for foot—burning for burning: wound for wound—bruise for bruise <note>This is the famous *Lex Talionis* or Law of Retaliation, intended to limit vengeance to a proportional punishment.</note>. **S**i if anyone strikes the eye of his male or female servant—and makes them one-eyed: he shall let them go free for the eye he plucked out. **D**entē also if he knocks out a tooth of his male or female servant: he shall likewise let them go free. **S**i if an ox strikes a man or woman with its horn—and they die—the ox shall be overwhelmed with stones: and they shall not eat its meat: and the owner of the ox shall be innocent. **Q**uod but if the ox was a gorer since yesterday and the day before <note>original: *nudiustertius*; a common Latin idiom meaning "the day before yesterday."</note>—and they warned its owner—and the owner did not shut it up—and it kills a man or woman: the ox shall be stoned: and they shall kill its owner. **Q**uod but if a price [a ransom] was imposed on him—he shall give for his life: whatever was demanded. **F**iliū also for a son and daughter—if the ox strikes them: he shall be subject to a similar sentence. **S**i if it attacks a male or female servant: he shall give thirty <term>shekels<note>original: *siclos*; a standard unit of weight and currency in the ancient Near East.</note></term> of silver to the master: but the ox shall be crushed with stones. **S**i if anyone opens a cistern and digs—and does not cover it—and an ox or donkey falls into it: the owner of the cistern shall restore the price of the beasts: but that which is dead—shall be his. **S**i if one man's ox wounds another's ox—and it dies: they shall sell the living ox and divide the price: and the carcass of the dead one—they shall divide between them. **S**i but if he knew that the ox was a gorer since yesterday and the day before and its owner did not guard it: he shall restore ox for ox: and shall receive the whole carcass. **c. xxii.**

**S**i if anyone steals an ox or a sheep—and kills or sells it: he shall restore five oxen for one ox: and four sheep for one sheep. **S**i if a thief is found breaking into a house or digging under it: and he dies from a wound received: the striker shall not be guilty of blood. **Q**uod but if he did this after the sun has risen—he has committed homicide: and he himself shall die. **S**i if he does not have what to restore for the theft: he himself shall be sold <note>into servitude to pay the debt of his theft.</note>. **S**i if what was stolen is found with him alive—whether ox—or donkey—or sheep: he shall restore double. **S**i if anyone damages a field or vineyard and lets his beast loose to graze on another's property: he shall restore whatever best things he has in his own field or vineyard as an estimation of the damage. **S**i if a fire breaking out finds thorns—and catches heaps of grain

<summary>This page outlines the "Covenant Code," detailing specific penalties for homicide, assault, and negligence, famously including the "eye for an eye" principle. It also addresses property laws regarding livestock and theft, establishing a framework for social justice and restitution.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the civil and religious laws of the Covenant (the Book of the Covenant) from the previous page. Having discussed personal injury, it now moves into property damage, theft, social justice for the vulnerable, and ritual obligations.</meta>

<header>Exodus.</header>

...or standing grain in the fields: the one who started the fire shall make full restitution. ¶ If anyone entrusts money or a vessel to a friend for safekeeping, and it is stolen from the one who received it: if the thief is found, he shall restore double. ¶ If the thief is hidden <note>meaning the thief is unknown or has not been caught</note>, the master of the house shall be brought before the gods <note>original: "ad deos"; in this legal context, the term refers to the judges or magistrates acting with divine authority</note> and shall swear that he did not stretch out his hand against his neighbor’s property to commit fraud, whether regarding an ox, a donkey, a sheep, clothing, or anything that can cause a loss. ¶ The case of both parties shall come before the gods: and if they judge him guilty, he shall restore double to his neighbor. ¶ If anyone entrusts to his neighbor an ox, donkey, sheep, or any beast for safekeeping, and it dies, is weakened, or is captured by enemies, and no one saw it: an oath shall be between them <note>literally "in the middle"</note> that he did not stretch out his hand to his neighbor's property; the owner shall accept the oath, and the other shall not be compelled to make restitution. ¶ But if it was taken by theft: he shall restore the loss to the owner. ¶ If it was eaten by a beast: let him bring that which was killed as evidence to him, and he shall not restore it. ¶ He who asks his neighbor for a loan of any of these things, and it is weakened or dies while the owner is not present: he shall be compelled to make restitution. ¶ But if the owner was present, he shall not restore it; especially if it had come hired for the price of its work <note>referring to a rented animal where the risk is factored into the fee</note>. ¶ If a man seduces a virgin who is not yet betrothed, and sleeps with her: he shall provide her dowry and have her as his wife. ¶ If the father of the virgin refuses to give her: he shall pay money according to the amount of the dowry which virgins are accustomed to receive. ¶ You shall not allow sorcerers <note>original: "maleficos"; often translated elsewhere as witches or practitioners of harmful magic</note> to live. ¶ Whoever lies with a beast: let him die the death. ¶ He who sacrifices to gods shall be put to death, except to the Lord alone. ¶ You shall not trouble a newcomer <note>original: "advenam"; a resident alien or foreigner</note>, nor shall you afflict him. ¶ For you yourselves were newcomers in the land of Egypt. ¶ You shall not harm the widow or the orphan. ¶ If you hurt them, they will cry out to me, and I will

hear their cry: and my fury will be kindled, and I will strike you with the sword; and your wives shall be widows, and your sons orphans. ¶ If you lend money to my people—the poor man who lives with you: you shall not press him like an extortioner <note>original: "exactor"; a debt collector or usurer</note>, nor oppress him with interest. ¶ If you take your neighbor’s garment as a pledge: you shall return it to him before the sun sets. ¶ For that is the only thing with which he is covered, the clothing for his flesh; nor has he anything else in which to sleep. ¶ If he cries out to me, I will hear him, because I am merciful. ¶ You shall not disparage the gods <note>here meaning the judges or divinely appointed authorities</note>, and you shall not curse the prince of your people. ¶ You shall not delay in offering your tithes and first-fruits. ¶ You shall give me the firstborn of your sons. ¶ You shall do likewise with your oxen and sheep. ¶ For seven days let it be with its mother; on the eighth day you shall return it to me. ¶ You shall be holy men to me. ¶ You shall not eat meat that has been tasted <note>pre-sampled or mangled</note> by beasts, but you shall throw it to the dogs. 

### Chapter 23

**N**on <note>This large initial marks the start of a new chapter</note> You shall not accept a voice of lies <note>original: "vocem mendacii"; a false report or rumor</note>: nor shall you join your hand to say false testimony for the wicked. ¶ You shall not follow a crowd to do evil: nor shall you yield to the opinion of the majority in a judgment so as to deviate from the truth. ¶ You shall not even pity a poor man in a judgment <note>meaning that justice must be impartial, not swayed even by sympathy for the poor</note>. ¶ If you encounter your enemy’s ox or wandering donkey: lead it back to him. ¶ If you see the donkey of one who hates you lying under its burden, you shall not pass by: but you shall lift it up with him. ¶ You shall not turn aside in the judgment of the poor. ¶ You shall flee from a lie. ¶ You shall not kill the innocent and the just: because I am an adversary to the wicked. ¶ Nor shall you accept bribes, which even blind the wise and subvert the words of the just. ¶ You shall not be troublesome to a stranger. ¶ For you know the souls of newcomers: because you yourselves were strangers in the land of Egypt. ¶ For six years you shall sow your land and gather its fruits: ¶ But in the seventh year you shall let it go and let it rest, so that the poor of your people may eat.

<summary>This page outlines laws concerning property restitution, social ethics—specifically the protection of widows, orphans, and foreigners—and the Sabbatical year for land. It emphasizes communal responsibility and impartial justice regardless of a person's status.</summary>

<meta>This page follows the ratification of the covenant at the base of Mount Sinai. Having established the laws of the covenant, the narrative now moves to the vision of God by the elders and Moses' ascent into the cloud to receive the stone tablets and instructions for building the Tabernacle.</meta>

<header>.Exodus.</header>

...obedient. He indeed sprinkled the blood upon the people and said: "This is the blood of the covenant which the Lord has made with you, according to all these words." And Moses and Aaron, Nadab and Abihu, and seventy of the elders of Israel ascended: and they saw the God of Israel. Under his feet there was, as it were, a work of sapphire stone <note>original: "lapidis saphirini"; a deep blue gemstone</note>, and like the sky when it is clear. And he did not stretch out his hand against those of the children of Israel who had withdrawn far off. And they saw God, and they ate and drank. Then the Lord said to Moses: "Come up to me on the mountain and stay there; and I will give you the two stone tablets and the law and the commandments which I have written, so that you may teach the children of Israel." Moses arose, and Joshua <note>original: "iosue"</note> his assistant; and as Moses was ascending the mountain of God, he said to the elders: "Wait here until we return to you. You have Aaron and Hur with you; if any dispute arises, you shall refer it to them." And when Moses had ascended, a cloud covered the mountain: and the glory of the Lord dwelt upon Sinai, covering it with a cloud for six days. But on the seventh day, the Lord called him from the midst of the darkness. Now the appearance of the glory of the Lord was like a burning fire upon the top of the mountain, in the sight of the children of Israel. And Moses, entering into the midst of the cloud, ascended the mountain: and he was there forty days and forty nights. 

### Chapter XXV.

**A**nd the Lord spoke to Moses, saying: "Speak to the children of Israel, that they may take offerings for me. From every person who offers of his own free will, you shall receive them. These, moreover, are the things you should receive: Gold, silver, and bronze; and hyacinth <note>likely blue or violet dyed wool</note>, purple, and twice-dyed scarlet, and fine linen; and goat hair, and rams' skins dyed red, and violet-colored skins, and acacia wood <note>original: "lignas sethim"; a durable wood used for the sacred furniture</note>: oil for preparing the lamps: spices for the ointment and sweet-smelling incense:

<column-break/>

onyx stones and gems to adorn the ephod <note>a ceremonial vestment worn by the high priest</note> and the breastplate <note>original: "rationale"</note>. And they shall make for me a sanctuary, and I will dwell in their midst: according to the complete pattern of the tabernacle that I will show you, and of all its vessels for its service. And thus shall you make it. Construct an ark <note>a sacred chest or "arca"</note> of acacia wood: whose length shall have two and a half cubits <note>a cubit is roughly the length of a forearm, about 18 inches</note>, the width one and a half cubits, and the height likewise one and a half cubits: and you shall overlay it with the purest gold inside and out. And you shall make a gold crown around it: and four gold rings, which you shall place at the four corners of the ark. Let two rings be on one side, and two on the other. You shall also make poles of acacia wood and cover them with gold. And you shall insert them through the rings which are on the sides of the ark, so that it may be carried by them. These shall always remain in the rings, nor shall they ever be pulled out of them. And you shall place in the ark the testimony <note>the stone tablets of the Law</note> which I will give you. You shall also make a propitiatory <note>original: "propiciatorium"; the "mercy seat" or cover of the Ark</note> of the purest gold. Its length shall be two and a half cubits, and the width one and a half cubits. You shall also make two golden cherubim <note>winged celestial beings</note> of hammered work on both sides of the oracle <note>the place where God's voice would be heard</note>. Let one cherub be on one side, and the other on the other. Let them cover both sides of the propitiatory, spreading their wings and covering the oracle: and let them face each other, with their faces toward the propitiatory with which the ark is to be covered: in which you shall put the testimony that I will give you. From there I will command and speak to you: namely, from above the propitiatory and from the middle of the two cherubim who shall be over the ark of the testimony, all things which I will command through you to the children of Israel. You shall also make a table of acacia wood, having two cubits of length, one cubit in width, and one and a half cubits in height: and you shall gild it with the purest gold. And you shall make for it a golden rim <note>original: "labium"; literally a "lip"</note> around it: and for that rim
